Q4: What emotion did Leo feel when he saw his future in the mirror?
Correct: wonder
Distractors: fear / anger / boredom
Explanation: The text says Leo felt wonder upon seeing his future. Fear, anger, or boredom are unsupported by the context.



Topic: a story about a magical pencil that brings drawings to life

Text:
Liam, a shy 12-year-old, found a strange pencil in his grandfather’s attic. It was old, with a silver tip that shimmered like moonlight. Curious, he drew a small cat on his notebook—and to his shock, the cat moved! It stretched, yawned, and even purred. Excited, Liam drew a cake with flour-dusted edges, and suddenly, the scent of vanilla filled the air. His little sister, Mia, baked a real cake using his sketch as a recipe. The staff at the school performance were amazed when Liam’s drawings of a drum set came to life, playing a lively rhythm.

But trouble followed. The mayor accused Liam of cheating on taxes because his drawings seemed too perfect. Embarrassed, Liam hid the pencil, but his account of the magic was ignored. The result? His drawings lost their magic—until one day, a lost puppy appeared in his sketch. The town, now attracted by the wonder, forgave him, and Liam learned that magic, like art, should be shared, not hidden.

Questions:
Q1: What did Liam’s first drawing with the magical pencil do?
Correct: It made the cat move and meow.
Distractors: It turned into a real cake / It played music like a drum / It disappeared after a few seconds
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states the cat "moved, stretched, yawned, and even purred," proving it came to life.
- Distractors:
  - "Turned into a real cake" is wrong because the cake was drawn later (Mia used the sketch to bake it).
  - "Played music like a drum" is incorrect—the drum was drawn in the school performance, not first.
  - "Disappeared after a few seconds" contradicts the cat’s active behavior (purring, moving).
---
Q2: Why did the mayor accuse Liam of cheating on taxes?
Correct: Because his drawings looked too perfect and seemed unreal.
Distractors: Because he refused to pay for the school performance / Because he used flour from the mayor’s store / Because the drum set broke during the performance
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says the mayor accused Liam of cheating on taxes because his drawings "seemed too perfect," implying they looked artificial (like magic).
- Distractors:
  - "Refused to pay for the school performance" is unsupported—the text doesn’t mention payments.
  - "Used flour from the mayor’s store" is irrelevant; the flour was in the cake drawing, not linked to taxes.
  - "The drum set broke" is false—the drum set played (came to life) during the performance.
---
Q3: What happened when Liam hid the magical pencil?
Correct: His drawings lost their magic.
Distractors: The pencil turned into dust / His sister Mia found it first / The mayor took it as evidence
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states, "His drawings lost their magic—until one day..." implying hiding the pencil caused the effect to stop.
- Distractors:
  - "Turned into dust" is never mentioned.
  - "Mia found it first" is false—Liam found it initially, and Mia only used his sketches.
  - "The mayor took it as evidence" is unsupported; the mayor only accused Liam, not seized the pencil.
---
Q4: How did the town react to Liam’s magic after the puppy incident?
Correct: They forgave him and accepted the wonder of his drawings.
Distractors: They demanded he perform magic for them daily / They banned him from drawing in public / They built a statue of the magical pencil
Explanation:
- Correct: The text says, "The town, now attracted by the wonder, forgave him," showing a positive reaction.
- Distractors:
  - "Demanded daily performances" is exaggerated—no such demand is stated.
  - "Banned him from drawing" contradicts the forgiving tone.
  - "Built a statue" is speculative and never mentioned.



Topic: a fairy tale about a woman who talks to birds and they help her

Text:
In a quiet village near a bustling port, there lived a woman named Elara, known for her strange relation with birds. While others found her odd, she could mention their names, and they would listen. One day, her mood darkened when a poisonous chemical spilled into the river, killing fish and scaring away animals. Elara sat by the shore, her breathing slow and heavy, as a flock of sparrows landed beside her. "We must act," chirped the eldest. "The humans won’t listen," she replied. The birds, determined to help, flew to the village square and convince the mayor to clean the river. Though the task was hard, their teamwork saved the land.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main problem in Elara’s village that the birds helped solve?
Correct: A chemical spill polluting the river
Distractors: A storm destroying crops / A disease spreading among animals / A fire in the forest
Explanation:
The text explicitly states that a poisonous chemical spilled into the river, killing fish and harming the environment. The other options (storm, disease, fire) are not mentioned in the story and contradict the details provided. The birds’ intervention directly addresses the pollution, making this the only correct answer.
---
Q2: How did the birds influence the mayor to act?
Correct: They flew to the village square and convinced him to clean the river.
Distractors: They sang a song that made him change his mind / They showed him a vision of the future / They threatened to leave the village
Explanation:
The text says the birds flew to the village square and convinced the mayor, using their relation with Elara to persuade him. The other options introduce elements not in the story (singing, visions, threats), which are plausible but unsupported by the text.
---
Q3: What was Elara’s emotional state before the birds helped her?
Correct: Her mood was dark and heavy.
Distractors: She was excited about the birds’ arrival / She was angry at the villagers / She was laughing at the situation
Explanation:
The text describes Elara’s mood as darkened when she sat by the river, with slow, heavy breathing, indicating sadness or worry. The other options (excitement, anger, laughter) contradict her described state and lack textual evidence.
---
Q4: Why did the birds decide to help Elara?
Correct: They realized humans wouldn’t listen otherwise.
Distractors: They felt sorry for her / They wanted to impress the villagers / They were ordered by a higher power
Explanation:
The text states the birds chirped, "We must act," because "the humans won’t listen," implying their relation with Elara and their frustration with human inaction. The other options (pity, impressing villagers, divine orders) are plausible but not supported by the narrative. The birds acted out of necessity, not emotion or external commands.



Topic: a story about protecting a magical forest from someone who wants to cut it down

Text:
In the southern part of the country, there was a magical forest called Eldermere, known for its valuable trees that glowed faintly at night. The local economy depended on tourism, but a powerful businessman, Mr. Veldrin, wanted to cut down the forest to build a resort. The villagers were confused—they loved the forest but needed money to survive.

Lena, a young guide who loved the outdoors, decided to act. She organized a meeting to improve communication between the villagers and the elders. Together, they created a plan: they would show Veldrin the forest’s true beauty and convince him to protect it. One evening, Lena led him on a hidden path through the trees, where fireflies lit their way. As they walked, Veldrin’s face softened. "This place is alive," he whispered. Lena smiled, relaxed now that he understood. The next day, Veldrin announced he would fund eco-tourism instead, ensuring Eldermere stayed safe for future generations.

Questions:
Q1: Why were the villagers confused about the forest?
Correct: They loved the forest but also needed money to support their economy.
Distractors: They didn’t know how to communicate with the elders / They were afraid of Mr. Veldrin / They wanted to cut down the forest themselves.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states they depended on tourism (economy) but also cherished the forest, creating confusion.
- Distractors:
  - "Didn’t know how to communicate" is false—they organized communication.
  - "Afraid of Veldrin" is unsupported; they acted despite him.
  - "Wanted to cut it down" contradicts their goal to protect it.
---
Q2: What did Lena do to help the villagers?
Correct: She organized a meeting to improve communication and led Veldrin on a path through the forest.
Distractors: She threatened Veldrin with legal action / She offered to sell the forest to him / She hid the forest’s magic from him.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text describes her role in improving dialogue and guiding Veldrin physically.
- Distractors:
  - "Threatened legally" is false—no conflict is mentioned.
  - "Offered to sell" contradicts the protection goal.
  - "Hid the magic" is wrong—she showed it to him.
---
Q3: How did Veldrin change his mind about the forest?
Correct: After seeing its beauty ahead on the path, he realized it was valuable beyond money.
Distractors: The elders convinced him with threats / Lena paid him to stop / The forest’s magic forced him to obey.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explains his emotional shift during the walk ("This place is alive").
- Distractors:
  - "Threats" are unsupported.
  - "Lena paid him" is false—no money exchange is mentioned.
  - "Magic forced him" is exaggerated; the text shows persuasion, not coercion.
---
Q4: What was the final outcome for Eldermere?
Correct: Veldrin funded eco-tourism to protect the forest instead of cutting it down.
Distractors: The villagers had to leave the forest / Veldrin built the resort anyway / The forest’s magic disappeared.
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states he "funded eco-tourism" as a solution.
- Distractors:
  - "Villagers left" is false—they stayed and benefited.
  - "Built the resort" contradicts the outcome.
  - "Magic disappeared" is unsupported; the forest remained magical.



Topic: a story about buying something online that looked completely different from the picture

Text:
Last week, I decided to buy a new coffee maker after seeing an advertisement with a sleek, modern design. The quotation on the website showed a beautiful machine in silver, but when my package arrived, I found a bulky, plastic model in bright orange. It looked nothing like the picture! At first, I felt a mix of emotion—anger, disappointment, and even embarrassment. I checked the category on the website again to confirm I had ordered the right product, but the description matched the orange one.

I tried to contact customer service for communication, but their response was slow. The representative said it was an unnecessary mistake and offered a refund. Still, I couldn’t help but stick my tongue out in frustration. The whole experience made me realize how important it is to read reviews carefully. Even worse, my friend later told me this was a common symptom of poor quality control in online shopping. I won’t be forced to buy from that site again!

Questions:
Q1: What was the main problem the writer faced with the coffee maker?
Correct: The product looked completely different from the advertisement.
Distractors: The coffee maker didn’t work properly / The website was difficult to navigate / The delivery was delayed.
Explanation: The text clearly states the product arrived in a different color and design than advertised. The other options are not mentioned—there’s no mention of functionality issues, navigation problems, or delays.
---
Q2: Why did the writer check the product category on the website?
Correct: To confirm they had ordered the correct item.
Distractors: To find a better price / To see if the product was in stock / To read customer reviews.
Explanation: The text explains the writer checked the category to verify the order details. The other options are irrelevant—price, stock, and reviews are not discussed in this context.
---
Q3: What emotion did the writer feel when they received the wrong product?
Correct: A mix of anger, disappointment, and embarrassment.
Distractors: Excitement and curiosity / Sadness and relief / Confusion and indifference.
Explanation: The text explicitly lists anger, disappointment, and embarrassment as the writer’s feelings. The other options contradict the text’s description of strong negative emotions.
---
Q4: What did the customer service representative offer as a solution?
Correct: A refund.
Distractors: A replacement product / A discount on the next purchase / Free shipping for future orders.
Explanation: The text states the representative offered a refund, not replacements, discounts, or free shipping. These options are plausible but unsupported by the text.



Topic: a story about a new app that made my life easier

Text:
Last month, I discovered an app called QuickHands that changed my daily routine slightly but for the better. Before, I had to pull heavy goods from the store, but now the app helps me organize deliveries. One day, I ordered groceries online, and the package arrived at my doorstep in just two hours—unlike the normal three-day wait! The delivery person even left a note saying, “Enjoy your meal!” I smiled, thinking how unfair it was that I used to spend hours shopping.

The brave linguists faced challenges, like shifting weather and limited resources, but their work ensures these languages survive. "Every word matters," said one expert, "even if only a handful of people used it yesterday."

Maria’s stories became so popular that she was invited to a writing program at Harvard.

My grandfather’s wisdom taught me that inspiration isn’t just about talent—it’s about courage and the willingness to shift perspectives. Without him, I wouldn’t have the confidence to challenge the status quo today.

His passion was infectious, and by the end of my trip, I was convinced that creativity had no limits—just like João’s imagination.

Mars, the "Red Planet," has long been a dream for explorers. A photographer on Mars might capture stunning sunsets, though the sky is pink due to dust. Meanwhile, Jupiter, the giant, has a curtain of storms, including the famous Great Red Spot. Saturn’s rings make it the most recognizable, but they’re not solid—they’re made of ice and rock.

Earth is the only planet with life, and humans consume its resources daily. Some argue we should be more grateful for our home. Beyond the planets, dwarf planets like Pluto remind us how much we still have to explore. Topic: an article about the unusual New Year traditions in different cultures

Text:

In Spain, eating twelve grapes at midnight is familiar, but in the Philippines, people wear cloth with red and yellow to scare away evil spirits. The range of colors symbolizes joy and protection. In Japan, temples ring bells 108 times to slow down the heart’s worries, while in Brazil, people jump seven waves at Copacabana Beach for good health. These traditions show how cultures create meaning from simple acts, even when they seem lonely or strange to outsiders. The basis of these customs often lies in ancient beliefs, blending history with modern fun.

Topic: an article about the most dangerous jobs in the world

Text:
Every year, experts make predictions about the most dangerous jobs in the world. These rankings often include professions where workers face high risks of injury or death. One of the most hazardous jobs is combining extreme conditions with physical labor, such as mining or fishing. In mining, workers descend into deep tunnels where accidents can occur suddenly, like cave-ins or explosions. Fishermen, on the other hand, battle rough seas and unpredictable weather, which can lead to shipwrecks or drowning.

Another dangerous field is transport, particularly for truck drivers or pilots. Long hours, fatigue, and mechanical failures increase the risk of crashes. Even office jobs can be risky—firefighters and police officers frequently put their lives on the line, while astronauts face threats from space debris or equipment malfunctions.

Interestingly, literature also highlights dangerous jobs. Books like The Count of Monte Cristo describe smugglers and pirates, whose performance under pressure was crucial for survival. Meanwhile, in modern times, jobs like oil rig workers or waste disposal workers deal with toxic materials, where a single mistake can have deadly consequences.

The average lifespan of workers in these professions is often shorter than the global average, proving how high the stakes are. Surprisingly, even seemingly safe jobs, like cashiers, can be dangerous if they handle large amounts of money, risking robbery. Safety receipts (training and equipment) are vital, but no amount of preparation can eliminate all risks. For example, a worker in a chemical plant might face exposure to harmful gases, while a deep-sea diver could get trapped in a pot of equipment failure.

Nearby, in the desert, lies the Garden of the Moon in Oman. Here, approximately 2,000 solar-powered lights illuminate a maze of sand dunes at night, creating a magical glow. While not all gardens are welcoming, these prove nature can be both wild and wonderful.

Topic: a story about a time I bought something valuable without realizing it

Text:
Last summer, I was working as a volunteer at a local photography exhibition. One day, an honest but familiar-looking gentleman approached me, asking if I could help him find a rare camera from the 1950s. He explained that his grandfather had given it to him, but he wanted to sell it to cover his medical bills. I wasn’t an expert, but I knew enough to recognize its value—it was a vintage Leica, worth thousands!

The gentleman asked if I’d like to buy it, offering a fair price. I hesitated, but he assured me it was better to sell it to someone who appreciated it. After some thought, I agreed. Later, I learned it was worth ten times more than he asked! My coach at work joked that I’d made a lucky wave—buying something priceless without realizing it. One easy method is to take deep breaths—inhale slowly, then exhale as if releasing a heavy liquid from a glass. This simple substance-based trick calms the mind instantly.

Another trick is to adjust your setting. A cluttered room can feel overwhelming, so tidying up even a small space can reduce stress. Try organizing items by size, like sorting grain (e.g., rice or pasta) into neat piles—it’s surprisingly satisfying.

For quick relief, try listening to calming music or nature sounds on a channel like YouTube or a meditation app. Even five minutes of soft sounds can ease tension. Finally, facing stress head-on works better than avoiding it. Write down worries, then knock them out one by one with actionable steps. Small efforts add up—stress doesn’t have to control your day!

Yet, some customers find the process annoying—waiting for deliveries, dealing with returns, or standing in a virtual till queue can be frustrating. Many people intend to shop online more often, but these challenges sometimes discourage them. In the following years, technology may improve, making online shopping even smoother.

The day we met, I led him down the familiar path to my garage, where the bike stood dusty and forgotten. It wasn’t just a bike; it was a reminder of a time I’d worked hard, even if the results hadn’t been perfect. My cousin promised to restore it and ride it proudly, just as I once had. Topic: an article about polar days and nights

Text:
In the Arctic and Antarctic regions, the sun never sets during the summer—a phenomenon called the polar day. For months, the sun stays above the horizon, giving equal daylight all the time. This is especially useful for outdoors activities like hiking or research, as there’s no need for artificial light. However, the opposite happens in winter: the polar night occurs, where the sun doesn’t rise at all. This can be challenging for viewers of wildlife or scientists studying the environment, as darkness lasts 24 hours.

The length of polar days and nights depends on how far north or south you are from the secondary circle (the Arctic or Antarctic Circle). Near the poles, the effect is more extreme. Some animals, like polar bears, have adapted to these conditions, but humans must plan carefully. For example, during polar nights, staying warm is practical—a thick coat can save you from a bite of the freezing wind. Tourists and researchers often use diagrams to track sunlight changes, helping them prepare for the long darkness.

Interestingly, polar regions have influenced history. In 1913, a Norwegian election for the Arctic Council highlighted the need for international cooperation to profit from scientific discoveries. Today, scientists judge these areas as vital for understanding climate change, proving that even extreme environments have global importance.

Questions:
Q1: What is the main advantage of polar days for people living or working outdoors?
Correct: Equal daylight for extended periods
Distractors: Longer sleep opportunities / No need for warm clothing / More time for elections
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states that during polar days, the sun stays above the horizon, providing equal daylight continuously, which is ideal for outdoor activities.
- Longer sleep opportunities is wrong because constant daylight would likely reduce sleep, not increase it.
- No need for warm clothing is incorrect—the text mentions cold conditions (e.g., "a bite of the freezing wind") require thick coats.
- More time for elections is unrelated; the text briefly mentions an election in 1913 but not as an advantage of polar days.
---
Q2: Why might a diagram be useful for someone experiencing polar nights?
Correct: To track the duration of darkness
Distractors: To predict animal migration patterns / To judge the quality of food / To profit from tourism
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explains that people use diagrams to track sunlight changes, which helps them prepare for the long darkness of polar nights.
- Predict animal migration patterns is plausible but not mentioned in the text; the focus is on human planning, not wildlife.
- To judge the quality of food is unrelated—the text discusses clothing and environmental conditions, not food.
- To profit from tourism is mentioned as a historical context but not as a practical use of diagrams for individuals during polar nights.
---
Q3: How do polar bears adapt to polar nights compared to humans?
Correct: They are naturally adapted, while humans need planning
Distractors: Both require thick coats / Humans adapt faster / Polar bears avoid the cold
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states that some animals (like polar bears) have adapted to polar conditions, whereas humans must plan carefully (e.g., using thick coats).
- Both require thick coats is partially true but oversimplifies—humans need planning beyond just clothing, while the text implies animals are inherently suited.
- Humans adapt faster contradicts the text, which suggests humans struggle more without preparation.
- Polar bears avoid the cold is incorrect; they thrive in cold environments, unlike humans who must actively cope.
---
Q4: What was the significance of the 1913 Norwegian election mentioned in the text?
Correct: It highlighted the need for international cooperation in Arctic research
Distractors: It decided how to profit from polar tourism / It judged the length of polar nights / It was a secondary event unrelated to science
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explains that the election was part of efforts to profit from scientific discoveries and judge the Arctic’s importance, emphasizing international cooperation.
- It decided how to profit from polar tourism is misleading—the text links the election to science, not tourism profits.
- It judged the length of polar nights is incorrect; the election was about broader cooperation, not measuring darkness.
- It was a secondary event unrelated to science is wrong—the text frames it as historically significant for climate research.



Topic: an article about an endangered species

Text:
Last year, scientists discovered a rare mystery in the Amazon rainforest—a tiny, blue-furred monkey they named Azul. Only 20 of these creatures were left, making them critically endangered. A team led by Dr. Elena Carter traveled to the forest to study them, hoping to save the species.

The researchers were filled with excitement when they first saw Azul’s group. However, their reaction changed when they noticed the monkeys were losing their habitat due to illegal logging. Dr. Carter explained, "We need to act fast—these monkeys are our content for conservation success."

To raise awareness, the team created a diagram showing the monkeys’ habitat and shared it with global organizations. They also released a photo album of Azul, which went viral. Many people responded with requests to help, including a famous musician who offered to export his concert profits to fund protection programs.

One night, a strange knock at their camp woke the team. They found a local villager who had seen the monkeys and wanted to help. His friendship with the researchers led to a new plan: training villagers to monitor the forest. Now, Azul’s future looks brighter than ever.

For example, the Arctic tern holds the record for the longest migration, flying over 15,000 kilometers between the Arctic and Antarctica—twice a year! Another odd fact is that some birds, like the pale swallow, have a similarity to ancient species found in literature, suggesting their migration patterns haven’t changed in thousands of years.

While migration is fascinating, it isn’t without disadvantages. Predators, storms, and human interference can disrupt their routes, sometimes forcing them to take unfashionable detours. Conservationists have launched campaigns to protect these routes, but urban development often annoys migrating birds by altering their usual paths. Text:
Nature has always been a romantic source of inspiration for inventors. For centuries, humans have studied animals, plants, and even weather patterns to solve problems. One famous example is the invention of the pan for cooking, which was inspired by the way ants carry food in their mandibles. This idea was so clever that it convinced early authorities to adopt it as a standard kitchen tool.

In contrast, some inventions have turned out worse than expected. For instance, early attempts to mimic bird flight led to clumsy machines that crashed till they were improved. Today, scientists use a process called biomimicry, where they copy natural designs. Approximately 1.8 million species have been studied, but only a fraction have been used in technology.

Even educated guesses sometimes fail. A famous case is the Velcro fastener, inspired by burrs sticking to clothing. The inventor, George de Mestral, spent years observing these plants before creating his album-sized prototype. His persistence paid off, proving that nature’s solutions are often the best.

Interestingly, in parts of Africa, visitors to a home may be asked to tip the host by bringing a small gift, like food or drink, as a sign of gratitude.

Even in modern settings, like university campuses, unusual rules persist. In Sweden, it’s customary to avoid eye contact with strangers to appear relaxing and non-threatening. Meanwhile, in some Middle Eastern countries, refusing an invitation to a home can be seen as an insult, so declining politely is an academic skill in itself.

The Luminara became a symbol of hope, and the legend was recorded in the final chapter of the book.

Topic: a personal story about living in a lighthouse

Text:
When I was a child, my family moved to a small island where the only job available was working in the lighthouse. It was an old type of lighthouse, built in the 19th century, with a spiral staircase that wound up to the top. The island was so quiet that the only sounds were the waves crashing against the rocks and the occasional cry of a seagull. It was silent in a way that made you feel both peaceful and a little lonely.

My father was the authority on the lighthouse—he knew every rule and every trick to keep it running smoothly. He even had a coach (a small rowboat) to take us to the mainland when supplies arrived. One day, a storm damaged a branch of the old oak tree near the lighthouse, and we spent hours cleaning up the mess. Naturally, my mother always prepared a hot meal in her cast-iron pan after long days of work.

The latest technology wasn’t allowed in the lighthouse—no radios, no phones—just an old lantern and a telescope. My father warned us never to cheat on our duties, like leaving the light unlit. The isolation taught me patience, and I still remember the smell of salt and the endless horizon.

The initiative, launched by a group of local settlers and environmentalists, focuses on reviving abandoned lots and converting them into green spaces. One of the key components of the project is the installation of solar-powered "eco-discs"—large, circular panels that not only generate clean energy but also serve as habitats for pollinators.

The project has faced criticism from some residents who find the eco-discs offensive to the city’s aesthetic, arguing they look like oversized discs cluttering the streets. However, Mayor Elena Rivera remains satisfied with the progress, stating that the project’s benefits far outweigh any visual concerns. To ensure transparency, the team has set up monitoring stations across the city, where volunteers can track air quality, biodiversity, and energy output in real time.

Despite initial skepticism, Green Haven has gained appeal among younger generations, who see it as a step toward a sustainable future. The project’s success has even inspired neighboring cities to adopt similar initiatives, proving that small changes can have a big impact.

Topic: a sci-fi story about space travel

Commander Elias Voss adjusted his graphics display, showing the industrial ruins of the abandoned space station orbiting the planet. "We have spare fuel for only one attempt," he said, his voice anxious but steady. "The last crew reported destruction from an unknown source before their signals cut off."

Lieutenant Mara Chen frowned at the holographic map. "The planet below looks tropical, but those energy readings suggest something far more deadly." She paused, pointing to a flickering anomaly near the equator. "If we land there, we risk triggering whatever wiped out the others."

Voss nodded. "We proceed with caution. No graphics or logs can prepare us for what’s waiting."

Topic: an adventure story about time travel

Text:

The device, covered in strange symbols, was entirely unlike anything he’d seen before. The screen flickered, and suddenly, he was standing in a lush forest where towering trees glowed with an eerie blue light. A creature unlike any species he knew approached him, its eyes reflecting the strange illumination. Liam had no time to panic—he had to defend himself, but the creature only bowed before him, as if waiting for a command.

Liam realized the console had transported him to another era, one where humans and alien lifeforms coexisted. A voice from the device offered him a proposal: he could pick one of three paths—restore balance to the timeline, uncover a lost interpretation of history, or return home immediately. Intrigued, Liam chose the second option, hoping to gain knowledge that would change humanity forever. As he explored, he found an ancient temple where walls were covered in glowing prints of events that hadn’t yet occurred in his world. The experience left him questioning reality, but also determined to share his discovery—if only he could find a way back.

Topic: a story about a cross-cultural misunderstanding

Text:
In the era of globalization, businesses often face challenges when expanding into new markets. One such case involved a Japanese company attempting to open a branch in a small town in Germany. The CEO, Mr. Tanaka, believed in neutral communication and decided to hire a local manager, Klaus, to help with the transition.

Klaus, proud of his ancestor’s traditions, insisted on a formal handshake greeting during meetings. However, Mr. Tanaka, accustomed to broad gestures like bowing, found this awkward. Their first attempt at a team-building event ended in confusion when Klaus expected a toast with beer, while Mr. Tanaka, mindful of budget constraints, offered only affordable juice.

The trouble escalated when Klaus suggested decorating the office with German flags, which Mr. Tanaka saw as unnecessary. Klaus, however, argued that it was a sign of respect. To accommodate both cultures, they compromised: the office walls were painted in a neutral gray, and a small flag was placed discreetly.

Afterwards, Mr. Tanaka realized that power dynamics in business were not just about hierarchy but also about cultural sensitivity. The experience taught him that even small gestures could bridge gaps if approached with understanding.

Correct: He was used to bowing instead of handshakes.
Distractors: He disliked formal greetings in general / He thought handshakes were too aggressive / He believed handshakes were impolite in Japan
Explanation: The text states Mr. Tanaka was accustomed to bowing, making handshakes unfamiliar. The distractors misrepresent his cultural background (e.g., assuming he dislikes all formal greetings or finds them aggressive, which contradicts the "neutral communication" approach). The last option is factually incorrect—handshakes are common in Japan but differ in context.
---
Q2: Why did Klaus insist on decorating the office with German flags?
Correct: He believed it was a sign of respect to local traditions.
Distractors: He wanted to assert dominance over the Japanese team / He thought it would make the office look more professional / He disliked the company’s neutral color scheme
Explanation: The text explicitly links Klaus’s flag suggestion to respect for ancestors’ traditions, not power struggles or aesthetics. The distractors introduce unrelated motives (dominance, professionalism, or color preferences), which are unsupported by the passage.
---
Q3: How did the company resolve the cultural conflict over drinks during the team-building event?
Correct: They compromised by offering affordable juice instead of beer.
Distractors: They canceled the event to avoid further misunderstandings / Mr. Tanaka insisted on serving beer despite the budget / Klaus agreed to skip the toast entirely
Explanation: The text describes a compromise (juice) due to budget constraints, not avoidance or insistence. The distractors contradict the collaborative tone (e.g., canceling the event or forcing beer) or misrepresent Klaus’s flexibility.
---
Q4: What did Mr. Tanaka learn about power dynamics in business after the experience?
Correct: They involve cultural sensitivity as much as hierarchy.
Distractors: Hierarchy is the only important factor in leadership / German managers always have more authority than Japanese ones / Small gestures like flags have no impact on teamwork
Explanation: The text highlights that Mr. Tanaka realized power extends beyond hierarchy to cultural understanding. The distractors oversimplify (e.g., hierarchy alone) or introduce false generalizations (e.g., German authority or flag irrelevance), which contradict the narrative’s emphasis on mutual respect.
---
Q5: Which word best describes the office’s final decoration compromise?
Correct: Neutral.
Distractors: Traditional / Luxurious / Controversial
Explanation: The text states the walls were painted neutral gray and flags were placed discreetly, avoiding extremes. "Traditional" implies cultural specificity (contradicting the compromise), "luxurious" is unrelated to the budget, and "controversial" is unsupported—the solution was pragmatic, not divisive.



Topic: an adventure story about an accidental invention

Text:
In the quiet coastal town of Eldermere, marine biologist Dr. Elena Vasquez was conducting a biological study on local algae when a dramatic accident changed her life forever. While testing a new filtration system at the research facility, she accidentally mixed two unique chemical compounds—one intended for water purification, the other a deliberate but unused byproduct of her experiments. The result was a gelatinous substance that, when exposed to sunlight, hardened into a translucent, ultra-strong material.

Elena initially dismissed it as a failure, but her curious workforce—a small team of engineers and chemists—saw potential. After months of trials, they refined the substance into a versatile polymer, later named "Luminite." Its properties were relevant to construction, medicine, and even art. The town’s mayor, impressed by the overall impact, declared the research facility a local monument, though Elena insisted the invention was purely accidental.

Today, Luminite is used in everything from bridges to surgical implants, proving that sometimes the most groundbreaking discoveries happen when you’re not looking. The story of Eldermere’s fully unexpected breakthrough now serves as a reminder: innovation doesn’t always require deliberate effort—sometimes, it just needs a little luck and a lot of curiosity.

Topic: a news article about the advantages and disadvantages of social media

Text:
Social media has become an unpredictable force in modern life, offering both opportunities and challenges. One major advantage is its ability to connect people globally, breaking down geographical obstacles and fostering communication. Businesses also benefit from platforms like Instagram and TikTok, where brands can sponsor ads to reach millions of users instantly. However, this convenience comes with risks. The curved nature of algorithms can amplify offensive content, making it difficult to control what users see. Additionally, misinformation spreads rapidly, often due to a lack of accuracy in shared posts. Many users face penalties for posting inappropriate material, but enforcement remains inconsistent. Besides, social media can encourage unhealthy comparisons, leading to anxiety. While it enhances connectivity, its downsides—such as privacy concerns and emotional strain—make it a double-edged sword. The key lies in using these platforms mindfully, avoiding mistakes like oversharing or engaging with toxic behavior.

Distractors: By increasing product prices / By eliminating the need for physical stores / By guaranteeing customer loyalty
Explanation: The text explicitly states that businesses use social media to sponsor ads for mass reach. The distractors are unrelated—pricing, physical stores, and loyalty are not mentioned as benefits.

Correct: Because they can amplify offensive content in an unpredictable way.
Distractors: Because they always show the same posts repeatedly / Because they require users to pay a fee / Because they block all negative comments automatically

Topic: a personal story about overcoming a significant fear

Text:
When I joined the local membership club at 16, I never expected it to change my life. The club offered a workshop on public speaking, which I attended out of curiosity—until I realized my fear of speaking in front of others was crippling. My voice would split into a whisper, and my hands would shake at the mere thought of being judged. The instructor, a former debate championship winner, noticed my struggle and suggested a gradual approach.

At first, I practiced in front of a mirror, then with small groups. The rate of my progress surprised even me. Within months, I delivered a speech at the club’s annual event, earning feedback that boosted my confidence. But my real test came during a drought in my social life. After a breakup, I isolated myself, convinced no one would understand my insecurities. The club’s efficient support system reminded me that vulnerability was strength.

Last year, I even helped manufacture a presentation tool for the club’s workshops—a wooden podium with a built-in microphone. Now, when I speak, I don’t just conquer fear; I inspire others to do the same.

Questions:
Q1: What initially motivated the author to attend the public speaking workshop?
Correct: Curiosity
Distractors: A requirement from school / Pressure from friends / A job application demand
Explanation: The text states the author attended "out of curiosity," not due to external pressure or obligations. The other options imply forced participation, which contradicts the voluntary nature described.
---
Q2: How did the instructor’s suggestion help the author overcome their fear?
Correct: By recommending a gradual approach
Distractors: By forcing immediate exposure / By ignoring the fear entirely / By replacing public speaking with writing
Explanation: The instructor’s "gradual approach" (mirror practice → small groups → events) is explicitly mentioned as the method. The other options contradict this structured, step-by-step strategy.
---
Q3: What event marked the author’s turning point in confidence?

The experience was significantly different from my usual routine, but in the best way possible. The goodness of a meal now competes with the fee for delivery, turning sustenance into a calculated expense. Topic: a personal account of a time I had to make a split-second decision

Text:
Last summer, I made a split-second decision that still feels like a investment in my future. While driving home from work, I noticed a car swerving erratically in front of me. My heart raced as I realized the driver—an elderly man—hadn’t noticed the red light. Without thinking, I slammed the brakes, but the pace of the oncoming traffic meant I couldn’t stop in time. The man’s car skidded toward me, and through my windshield’s lens, I saw his face frozen in panic.

In that shocking moment, I made a choice: I swerved sharply into the opposite lane, narrowly missing a truck. My licence to drive had always been a source of pride, but now it felt like a fraud—how could I have been so reckless? The elderly man’s car crashed into the guardrail, but he walked away with minor injuries. Later, I learned he’d been taking medication that affected his reflexes, explaining his erratic driving.

The transition from fear to relief was instant. I pulled over, shaking, and called the police. The man thanked me, saying I’d saved his life. That day, I realized split-second decisions aren’t just about luck—they’re about instinct, training, and sometimes, sheer zone of clarity. I’ll never forget how that moment changed me.

Topic: a story about an experience that completely changed my perspective on life

Text:
The summer I turned twenty-one, I decided to volunteer at a local wildlife sanctuary as a way to escape my predictable routine. My friends called it a waste of time, but I was partly motivated by curiosity—what if helping animals could boost my perspective on life? The job involved cleaning enclosures, preparing food, and, most unexpectedly, documenting the behavior of rescued birds. At first, I saw myself as just another spectator in their world, but soon, I realized how deeply connected we all were.

One day, a wounded eagle arrived. The vet said it might not survive, but the sanctuary’s team refused to give up. They worked tirelessly, and against all speculation, the eagle recovered. Watching its extension of wings for the first time in weeks was a turning point. I felt a strange incentive to change my own life—if this bird could overcome such hardship, why couldn’t I? That experience taught me that life isn’t bound by our fears or past mistakes. It’s about choices, and I decided to elect a path less traveled.

Now, I study environmental science, and every day, I’m reminded of that eagle. The lesson? Even small acts of kindness can reshape your worldview.

Correct: Partly curiosity about whether helping animals could boost her perspective on life
Distractors: A desire to impress her friends / A requirement for her university degree / A forced community service sentence
Explanation: The text states she was "partly motivated by curiosity" and mentions wanting to "boost my perspective on life." The other options are unsupported—her friends doubted her choice, there’s no mention of university requirements, and she wasn’t forced.

Topic: a story about a time I failed spectacularly and what I learned

Text:
Last summer, I decided to buy a classic guitar to learn a new tune for a national music festival. Confident in my skills, I skipped the extensive research most musicians do and instead relied on a friend’s vague gesture of approval. My mistake became clear when I arrived at the store and realized the instrument was on sale only for a limited discount—but the price tag had been crossed out with a marker, leaving me in total confusion. The salesperson, amused by my hesitation, pointed out that the original price was actually higher, but the discount had been misapplied.

Panicked, I agreed to the inflated price, only to later discover the guitar was defective. The strings snapped during my first practice session, and the housing for the pickups was damaged. The store refused to refund me, claiming the discount was a "one-time offer." Humiliated, I had to sell the guitar at a loss just to break even.

This experience taught me that even small oversights can lead to plus (and minus) consequences. Now, I always double-check details—whether it’s a price, a contract, or a friend’s advice—because one gesture of approval isn’t enough to avoid disaster.

The original price wasn’t higher than advertised (it was lower with the misapplied discount), and negotiation isn’t mentioned.

Explanation: The narrator concludes that even minor mistakes (like skipping research) can have plus (and minus) consequences, emphasizing caution. Explanation: The text states the store refused to refund the narrator, using the discount as an excuse. The other options contradict the text’s details—no replacement, partial refund, or manufacturer blame is mentioned.

Explanation: The phrase highlights that the narrator’s mistake led to both positive (learning a lesson) and negative (financial loss) results. Among them was a young journalist named Clara, who had once dreamed of uncovering the truth behind the world’s greatest invasion—not of armies, but of forgotten stories. One afternoon, while organizing a distribution of old manuscripts, Clara stumbled upon a letter addressed to her. The handwriting was unmistakable—it belonged to Daniel, her childhood friend, the one she had lost touch with after a risky decision years ago. The letter read: "I found a bargain in an antique shop—a first-edition book signed by a lord who once inspired revolutions. I thought you’d appreciate it. Let’s meet."

Clara hesitated. Reconnecting felt like stepping into uncharted territory, but the curiosity won. They met at a café near the river, where Daniel explained how he had incorporated his passion for history into his work as a tour guide. "I never satisfied myself with just talking about the past," he admitted. "I wanted to bring it to life." Clara smiled, realizing some connections, like old books, only reveal their value over time.

My speculation was that he didn’t care about the environment, but his arrogance made it worse.

I couldn’t stay silent. I approached the group, explaining how their actions were wrong and how pollution harmed marine life. The guide, however, dismissed me, saying, "We’re just having fun—don’t ruin the vibe." Frustrated, I turned to the other tourists, hoping to find unity in our shared concern. Surprisingly, a few nodded in agreement, but most avoided eye contact, fearing conflict.

As we docked, I realized this was just one phase of a larger problem—people prioritizing convenience over sustainability. The man who littered didn’t even leave his name, but his carelessness stuck with me. Now, I volunteer for beach cleanups, determined to make a difference.

Topic: a story about the day I decided to quit something that was no longer serving me

Text:
Last summer, I made a critical decision that changed my life. For years, I had been part of a small minority of people obsessed with skydiving, a passion that once filled me with joy. But after a crash during a training jump, I realized something fundamental had shifted. The fear no longer felt like excitement—it hurt, and worse, it was holding me back. My instructor warned that my hesitation could lead to failure in future jumps, and I knew I couldn’t ignore it anymore.

The final straw came when I watched a jet pilot from a nearby airbase give a talk about discipline. He mentioned how even the best pilots must accept limits, and that pushing beyond them could turn a thrill into an offence against safety. His words struck a chord. Moreover, I realized I wasn’t just afraid of failing—I was afraid of not failing, of letting others down by exceeding my comfort zone in ways that weren’t sustainable.

That evening, I stood at the edge of the drop zone and whispered, “I quit.” It wasn’t about giving up; it was about reclaiming my peace. The relief was instant. I’ve since taken up hiking, where the risks are manageable, and the joy is just as real—without the weight of self-doubt.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I understood a piece of advice I had ignored for years

One evening, exhausted, I attended a neutral workshop on productivity. The speaker, a former engineer, shared a shocking statistic: A trillion hours of wasted time globally could fund a new economy. That moment sparked my creativity. I stopped micromanaging tasks and instead focused on efficient systems—automating repetitive work, delegating, and prioritizing deep focus.

The shift was transformative. What I’d once seen as lazy became survival. That instance taught me that intelligence isn’t just about effort but about leveraging tools and mindset. Now, I advocate for this principle, though I still catch myself falling back into old habits.

Topic: a story about a time I broke a family tradition and the consequences

Text:
Last summer, I decided to abandon a cherished family tradition that had been passed down for generations. Every year, my grandparents would host a gathering where all their children—my parents and my uncle—would meet to discuss the future of our small business, almost like a mini parliament. The tradition was sacred, and my sibling and I were expected to attend, but I had other plans.

I had always perceived this tradition as outdated, especially since my parents relied on outdated transportation methods for their business. When my grandparents announced the annual meeting, I told them I couldn’t attend, claiming a prior commitment. My vision for the business was different—I wanted to modernize it, and I believed skipping the meeting would show my independence. However, my confusion grew when my parents didn’t react with anger but with disappointment. They said they understood my worst fears—that I was rejecting our roots—but they also warned me of the consequences.

A week later, my uncle, who had always been the family’s dealer in negotiations, offered me a job in the business. But it came with a condition: I had to attend the next gathering and present my ideas. I realized then that my parents hadn’t abandoned me; they had given me a chance to forgive myself for my impulsive decision. The tradition wasn’t just about meetings—it was about unity, and I had to earn my place back.

Questions:
Q1: What was the main reason the narrator decided to skip the family gathering?

Topic: a personal reflection on what it means to live a good life outside the metrics of productivity

Text:
What does it mean to live a good life? For centuries, societies have cited productivity as the ultimate measure—how much we earn, how rapidly we climb the career grade, or how efficiently we fill our days. But in an era of mass burnout and constant moving targets, this definition feels hollow. My origin story, like many, was shaped by the belief that success meant outworking others, defeating rivals, and proving my worth through tangible achievements. Yet, over time, I realized this pursuit was convincing only until it left me exhausted.

A crucial shift came when I met an elderly neighbor who spent her days gardening, reading, and hosting tea parties. She had no grade in life’s hierarchy, yet her days were rich with meaning. She taught me that a good life isn’t about maximizing output but nurturing what truly values us—relationships, curiosity, and quiet joy. Productivity metrics ignore the origin of our desires: Do we want to be busy, or do we want to be moving toward something that feeds our soul? The answer, for me, lies in the latter. The world rewards speed and scale, but fulfillment often hides in the unscheduled moments, where we’re not defeating time but savoring it.

Topic: an essay on the psychological impact of doomscrolling and the need for a new digital literacy

Text:
In the age of smartphones, a new psychological phenomenon has emerged—doomscrolling, the compulsive consumption of negative news. While it may seem harmless, research suggests it has spoiled our mental well-being, leaving many feeling anxious and overwhelmed. The pure irony lies in how we seek information to stay informed but end up drowning in despair. Psychologists and therapists warn that this habit disrupts sustainable emotional balance, much like an aircraft losing altitude due to turbulence.

The problem isn’t just the content but our inadequate digital literacy. Many users lack the skills to navigate news feeds critically, making them easy prey for sensationalism. Critics argue that social media algorithms act as opponents, feeding us more of what keeps us scrolling—even if it’s distressing. However, the solution isn’t to abandon technology but to develop adequate strategies. For instance, setting time limits or seeking feedback from trusted sources can mitigate harm.

The psychological toll is evident: studies show doomscrolling increases stress hormones, while reducing productivity. Yet, the cycle persists because we mistake engagement for connection. Just as a single feather can disrupt a calm lake, small doses of negativity can ripple into long-term anxiety. The key is awareness—recognizing when we’re consuming news passively and choosing sustainable habits instead.

Distractors: It enhances emotional resilience over time / It has no measurable impact on mental health / It primarily benefits productivity
Explanation: The text explicitly states that doomscrolling "disrupts sustainable emotional balance," using the aircraft metaphor to illustrate instability. The other options contradict the evidence: resilience isn’t mentioned, no impact is false (studies show harm), and productivity is reduced, not benefited.

Topic: an analysis of the psychological principles behind effective user interface design

Text:
The design of user interfaces (UIs) is not just about aesthetics—it relies heavily on psychological principles to create intuitive and efficient systems. A senior researcher in human-computer interaction once argued in their thesis that the brain processes visual material more efficiently when elements follow specific cognitive rules. One key principle is proportion: screens that balance white space and content reduce shock to the user’s visual organ, preventing overload. Studies show that users retain information better when interfaces minimize short-term memory strain, such as through clear hierarchies and familiar icons.

A recent survey of tech professionals revealed that 78% of designers prioritize finding quick navigation paths, aligning with the league of usability experts who emphasize reducing cognitive friction. For example, placing critical actions within the "F-pattern" (left-aligned, top-heavy layouts) leverages how users naturally scan pages. The finding that consistency in design patterns (e.g., button placement) speeds up task completion underscores the importance of predictable interactions. Ultimately, effective UI design bridges psychology and technology, ensuring users feel in control rather than frustrated.

- They force users to focus on short-term memory tasks.

The distractors are plausible but unrelated: attractiveness to designers (not mentioned), material quality (irrelevant), and forcing short-term memory tasks (contradicts the text’s goal of reducing such strain).

The atmosphere is equally intentional. Dim, warm lighting casts a cozy glow over wooden tables, while soft jazz floats from hidden speakers. The menu offers multiple tasting options, from a six-course degustation to a simpler three-course meal, proving the restaurant caters to diverse tastes. Even the adequate pricing (considering the quality) felt justified after tasting the perfectly balanced dishes.

What impressed me most was the restaurant’s subtle storytelling. A chalkboard near the bar lists the day’s "hunt" finds, and the sommelier explains how each wine was chosen to complement the chef’s vision. It’s not just a meal; it’s an experience that respects tradition while embracing innovation.

Topic: a reflective essay on the experience of impostor syndrome in a new professional or social role

Text:
Stepping into my first role as a chair of the university’s debate club was both thrilling and terrifying. I had spent years preparing—reading every edition of debate handbooks, practicing speeches until my voice cracked, and even training with a bat (yes, a real one, to improve my reflexes). Yet, as the grand opening night approached, doubt crept in. Was I truly qualified? The feeling of impostor syndrome shaped my early days like an unseen hand, whispering that I was a fraud despite the foundation of my experience.

At first, I dismissed it as nerves. But furthermore, every time I sat at the chair, I expected someone to reveal my lack of expertise. The tragedy wasn’t just the fear—it was how it stifled my confidence. I’d spend hours rehearsing, only to second-guess myself mid-sentence. Then, one evening, a student delighted me by saying, “You make it look effortless.” That moment was a turning point. I realized impostor syndrome wasn’t a flaw in my abilities but a component of growth—a signal to embrace vulnerability rather than hide it.

Over time, I learned to reframe the doubt. Instead of seeing it as proof of inadequacy, I treated it as a reminder to stay humble and keep learning. The debate club became more than a role; it became a space where my imperfections were met with encouragement. Now, when I hear that familiar voice of self-doubt, I smile. It’s not gone, but it no longer controls me. The foundation of my journey was laid not in perfection, but in the courage to sit in the chair anyway.

Questions:
Q1: What initially triggered the author’s impostor syndrome in their new role?
Correct: The expectation that someone would expose their lack of expertise.
Distractors: The physical training with a bat / The university’s debate handbooks / The student’s compliment about effortlessness.
Explanation:
The correct answer is supported by the text’s description of the author’s fear of being “revealed” as unqualified (“every time I sat at the chair, I expected someone to reveal my lack of expertise”). The bat training and handbooks were preparation tools, not triggers. The student’s compliment came after the syndrome took hold, not before.
---
Q2: How does the author describe the “tragedy” of impostor syndrome in the text?
Correct: It stifles confidence and prevents self-expression.
Distractors: It leads to physical exhaustion / It causes students to lose interest / It forces people to quit their roles.
Explanation:
The text states the “tragedy” is how the syndrome “stifled my confidence” and made the author “second-guess myself mid-sentence”. The other options are unsupported—no mention of exhaustion, student disinterest, or quitting.
---
Q3: What does the author learn to reframe about impostor syndrome?
Correct: It is a signal to embrace vulnerability rather than hide it.
Distractors: It proves they are not cut out for leadership / It should be ignored completely / It only affects beginners.
Explanation:
The author explicitly states they “learned to reframe the doubt” as “a signal to embrace vulnerability” (“a reminder to stay humble and keep learning”). The other options contradict the text’s positive reinterpretation.
---
Q4: Which word from the text suggests that the author’s experience was part of a larger, evolving process?
Correct: Shaped
Distractors: Foundation / Component / Delighted
Explanation:
“Shaped” implies ongoing influence (“impostor syndrome shaped my early days”), while foundation refers to a starting point, component to a part of a whole, and delighted to a single emotional reaction. Only shaped conveys development over time.
---
Q5: What does the author ultimately realize about their imperfections in the debate club?
Correct: They are met with encouragement rather than judgment.
Distractors: They are a sign of weakness / They disappear with experience / They make the role impossible.
Explanation:
The text describes imperfections as met with “encouragement” (“a space where my imperfections were met with encouragement”). The other options are contradicted—imperfections are framed positively, not as weakness or impossibility.

Topic: a story about a portrait painter who realizes they are capturing the subject's soul

Text:
Eleanor Hart had spent years perfecting her craft as a portrait painter, but something always felt incorrect—until the day she met Daniel Mercer. A newly promoted conductor with a reputation for brilliance, Daniel was also a man haunted by the loss of his younger brother. Eleanor’s studio, bathed in soft, golden lighting, became their sanctuary. She dared to paint him not just as he appeared, but as he was—raw, vulnerable, and stunning in his honesty.

The first sketches were cold, technical. But on the third session, Daniel’s hands trembled as he spoke of his brother’s death. Eleanor hesitated, then adjusted her palette. She painted his eyes not as they flickered in the high ceiling lights, but as they glowed with unshed tears. The portrait took on a life of its own, as if Daniel’s soul had seeped into the canvas. When he saw it, his breath caught. “You’ve captured more than my face,” he whispered. “You’ve satisfied something inside me.”

Eleanor realized then that great art wasn’t about perfection—it was about daring to see the unique truth in another. She never resigned herself to ordinary brushstrokes again. That portrait, now in a private collection, is said to hum when played near a piano, as if the organ of Daniel’s grief still resonates through the colors.

Questions:
Q1: What initially made Eleanor’s early portraits of Daniel feel unsatisfying to her?
Correct: They lacked emotional depth and authenticity.
Distractors: They were technically flawed. / They used the wrong color palette. / They were too similar to her previous works.
Explanation:
The text states Eleanor’s first sketches were “cold, technical,” implying a lack of emotional connection. The correct answer reflects this—her work felt incorrect because it missed the subject’s soul. The distractors are plausible (technical flaws, wrong colors, or repetition) but the text emphasizes the emotional disconnect, not the execution.
---

Correct: Daniel revealed his vulnerability about his brother’s death.
Distractors: The studio lighting changed unexpectedly. / She realized her previous technique was outdated. / Daniel requested a different style.
Explanation:
The text specifies that Daniel’s hands trembled as he spoke of his brother, and Eleanor “hesitated, then adjusted her palette.” This shows his emotional disclosure prompted her change. The distractors are red herrings: lighting isn’t mentioned as a factor, her technique wasn’t outdated, and Daniel didn’t direct her—she dared to go deeper on her own.

Distractors: It shocked him into silence. / It reminded him of his brother. / It made him question her artistic choices.
Explanation:

The correct answer captures this emotional fulfillment. Correct: It is said to hum when played near a piano, as if Daniel’s grief still resonates.
Distractors: It has been stolen and never recovered. / It changes color depending on the viewer’s mood. / It is displayed in a museum where it attracts crowds daily.

The distractors introduce unrelated details: theft, mood-dependent colors, and museum fame are not mentioned. The correct answer reflects the supernatural or symbolic resonance implied by the text’s metaphor of the “organ” of grief.

"A child’s photo could’ve been swapped accidentally."

Daniel, now a freelance graphic designer, earns a modest wage but struggles with the mystery. "It’s unsettling," he admitted. "But if it’s not me, who is it? And why does he look so much like me?"

Topic: an adventure story about teenagers who find themselves inside a computer game

Text:

Mia groaned, “We’re not accountants!” Yet, their conservative upbringing had taught them logic, and they cracked the code. Topic: a story about a difficult ethical dilemma I faced

Text:
Last summer, I faced a dynamic ethical dilemma that left me hesitate for weeks. As a volunteer at a homeless shelter, I noticed a young man, Jake, who often slept naked on the cold concrete floor, refusing help. One night, I found him shivering, his body bent from exhaustion. The shelter rules were predictable: no exceptions for warmth or privacy, but something inside me urged me to break them.

A narrow hallway separated Jake’s space from the women’s dormitory, where a group of volunteers, including my close friend Maria, worked alongside me. I knew Maria would disapprove—she believed in strict rules—but the guilt of leaving Jake to suffer was overwhelming. When I finally gathered the courage to ask the manager for a blanket, she refused, saying, “We can’t impose our values on others.” Yet, as I walked away, a new dilemma arose: Should I steal a blanket, risking my job, or let Jake freeze?

That night, I made a choice. I took an old scarf from the lost-and-found and covered him. The next morning, Jake was gone. Maria never found out, but the experience taught me that ethics aren’t always predictable—sometimes, the right path is the one that feels most human.

Distractors: Jake had asked them not to interfere / The narrator was afraid of getting fired / Jake was aggressive toward volunteers
Explanation:
The text states the shelter had “predictable” rules with “no exceptions,” forcing the narrator to hesitate. The other options are incorrect: Jake did not ask to be left alone (he was shivering), fear of firing was a secondary concern (not the main reason for hesitation), and there’s no mention of aggression.

Correct: An internal urge pushed them to act despite the rules.
Distractors: Maria directly blamed them for not helping / Jake threatened to report them / The narrator had promised Jake they would help.
Explanation:
The text says, “something inside me urged me to break [the rules].” Maria’s disapproval was implied but not stated as a cause of guilt; Jake made no threats; and there’s no mention of a prior promise.
---
Q3: What did the narrator do to help Jake secretly?
Correct: They took a scarf from the lost-and-found and covered him.
Distractors: They asked Maria to intervene / They called an ambulance / They gave Jake their own jacket.
Explanation:
The narrator “took an old scarf from the lost-and-found”, not Maria’s intervention, an ambulance, or their own jacket. These options contradict the text’s details.
---
Q4: How did the narrator’s friend Maria likely feel about the narrator’s decision?
Correct: She would have disapproved of breaking the rules.
Distractors: She supported the narrator’s choice / She reported the narrator to management / She helped cover up the incident.
Explanation:
The text notes Maria “believed in strict rules” and implies she would disapprove. There’s no evidence she supported the narrator, reported them, or helped cover it up.
---
Q5: What lesson did the narrator learn from this experience?
Correct: Ethics aren’t always predictable—sometimes the right path is the most human one.
Distractors: Rules should always be followed without question / Helping others is more important than consequences / The shelter’s policies were unfair but unchangeable.
Explanation:
The narrator concludes, “ethics aren’t always predictable—sometimes, the right path is the one that feels most human.” The other options misrepresent the text: the narrator doesn’t claim rules should always be broken, nor that consequences don’t matter, nor that policies were unchangeable.



Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was no longer a child

Text:
The moment I realized I was no longer a child came during my first summer job at my uncle’s settler farm. At twelve, I had spent years helping with small tasks—milking cows, fixing broken fences, or sorting eggs from the dairy—but this time felt different. My uncle handed me a half-finished quilt, its fabric a patchwork of colors, and said, "This needs a reduction in chaos. Turn it into something coherent."

I stared at the tangled threads, overwhelmed by the confusion of patterns. My childhood creation had always been simple: drawings, stories, or building forts. But this? It demanded precision. That night, I stayed up late, separating threads by hue, testing combinations until the design emerged. By dawn, the quilt was a masterpiece—not because of my skill, but because I’d accepted the weight of responsibility.

Years later, I understood: that quilt was my thesis on growing up. The finding wasn’t just in the stitches but in the realization that childhood’s carefree creation had given way to something deeper—a quiet, stubborn maturity.

Topic: a personal essay on the biggest misconception people have about me

People assume I’d never take out a loan, but I once borrowed money to start an organic farm on the side—a project I’m proud of, even if it didn’t pay off immediately. I even sent a heartfelt apology to my friends when the farm failed, but they understood. Overall, I think the key to breaking these stereotypes is honesty. Topic: a story about a time I had to navigate a complex bureaucratic system

Text:

I needed to declare my income for a visa extension, but the process was far from straightforward. After weeks of back-and-forth emails and phone calls, I finally received a resolution. The latest update to the system had been rolled out just days before, and my case was one of the first to be processed under the new rules. Though exhausting, the experience taught me resilience—and a healthy dose of skepticism toward paperwork.

Topic: a personal story about a time I felt like an outsider and how I coped

One day, a group of boys accused me of not belonging, saying I was "too different" to fit in. It stung, but what hurt more was realizing how racial stereotypes had shaped their perception of me.

My parents, though supportive, didn’t fully understand the moral weight of the situation. They urged me to ignore the comments, but I needed more than that. I turned to books—stories of immigrants and outsiders who found their place by embracing their uniqueness. I also joined the school’s debate club, where my voice became relevant in discussions. Over time, I learned to alter my approach: instead of trying to blend in, I focused on what made me stand out. When our school launched a diversity project, I took the lead, proving that my differences weren’t weaknesses but strengths. The boys who once mocked me now asked for my help. It wasn’t easy, but I realized that fitting in wasn’t the goal—being appropriate to myself was.

I later shared his story with my sister, who had been distant after a minor quarrel. We laughed, cried, and finally put our pride aside. Topic: a story about a time I discovered a secret that changed my perception of my family

Text:
Last summer, while helping my senior uncle repair an old steam engine in his garage, I stumbled upon something unexpected. Between rusted tools and faded blueprints, I found a hidden box tucked behind a loose panel. Inside were yellowed letters, a protein-rich meal plan, and a diary detailing my grandfather’s secret tendency to experiment with alternative medicine in the 1970s. At first, I dismissed it as curiosity, but as I read further, I realized this wasn’t just history—it was a bet my grandfather had made with my grandmother: to prove that natural remedies could heal her chronic pain, despite the family’s assurance that modern medicine was the only solution.

The timing of this discovery was eerie. My grandmother, now frail, had recently mentioned how my grandfather’s old recipes had helped her sleep better. I pieced together that his experiments had worked, but he’d kept it quiet, fearing judgment. Moreover, the diary revealed he’d sought assistance from a local herbalist, someone no one in the family knew about. This secret explained why my grandmother, though skeptical of unconventional methods, had a tendency to slip traditional teas into my childhood meals—“just in case.”

That night, I confronted my grandmother. She confessed that my grandfather’s silence wasn’t defiance but love—he’d wanted to assure her that his methods were safe before revealing them. The discovery reshaped my view of my family: they weren’t rigid traditionalists but people who valued science and intuition, who loved extensively but quietly. The steam from the engine that day had cleared more than the garage’s dust—it had unveiled a legacy of courage and care I’d never seen before.

He recalled his uncle’s habit of hiding clues in plain sight. After weeks of frustration, he finally pried it open. Inside wasn’t a portrait or a lock of hair, but a tiny map—drawn on brittle paper—leading to a forgotten cemetery where his uncle had once sworn he’d buried something "more precious than gold."

Topic: a story about a time I had to live with a difficult person and what it taught me

Text:
Last summer, I took a full-time job as a live-in caregiver for an elderly woman named Mrs. Thompson. At first, I was excited—she seemed kind, and the pay was generous. However, within days, I realized she was one of the most difficult people I’d ever met. She had expectations that were impossible to fulfill: she wanted me to adjust her meals to her ever-changing tastes, trade her medications at odd hours, and even rearrange her furniture without warning. Her demands were pure chaos, and I often felt like I was solving a puzzle with missing pieces.

What made it worse was her humorous yet passive-aggressive remarks. She’d laugh while criticizing my cooking, saying, “This is adequate… for a beginner,” as if I were still in training. I wanted to oppose her behavior, but I knew I couldn’t afford to quit. Instead, I started speculating about how to turn the experience into a lesson.

One evening, after a particularly exhausting day, I snapped and asked why she treated me this way. She sighed and admitted she was lonely and feared being abandoned. That moment changed everything. I stopped seeing her as a difficult person and instead focused on being patient and kind. By the end, I’d learned that even the toughest challenges can teach empathy—something no textbook could.

Topic: a fantasy story about a librarian who discovers the books can rewrite themselves

Text:
In the quiet town of Eldermere, where the mist clung to the cobblestones like an old blanket, lived Elias Veyne, a librarian known for his steady hands and even steadier temper. For years, he had acquired rare manuscripts, ensuring the town’s library remained a treasure trove of knowledge. But one evening, as he shelved a newly donated tome, he noticed something peculiar: the pages seemed to rewrite themselves, the ink shifting like liquid silver. Intrigued, Elias traced the strange phenomenon to a single cell of text—a phrase that had appeared overnight. His optimistic nature led him to believe this was no trick of the light, but a discovery of unimaginable power.

The following morning, Elias elected to test his theory. He left the book open on his desk and accompanied it with a journal, recording every change. To his astonishment, the text evolved, switching between interpretations of the same story, as if the book itself debated its own meaning. By week’s end, Elias had uncovered a pattern: the book responded to the reader’s emotions, reshaping its narrative to reflect their deepest desires—or fears. Though the town’s scholars dismissed his claims as madness, Elias refused to abandon his quest. If books could rewrite themselves, what else might the library hide?

Topic: an adventure story about a mapmaker charting a continent that reshapes itself constantly

Text:
The year was 1893, and Elias Voss, a renowned but eccentric mapmaker, had just received a commission that would either make him famous or drive him to ruin. The governor of the newly discovered continent of Fluxis had sent a desperate telegram: "Your maps are the only ones that keep up with us. Please come—our land shifts daily." Skeptical but intrigued, Elias packed his tools and sailed west.

Upon arrival, he met the governor’s aide, a nervous young man who whispered, "The last cartographer was found dead near the Whispering Dunes. A local gang claims they protect the land’s secrets." Elias scoffed—until he saw the maps. Rivers that had been dry for weeks now snaked through valleys, and mountains that had stood firmly for centuries had vanished overnight. His first attempt to chart the coastline ended in chaos when a sudden earthquake split the shore in two. "An incident," the locals muttered, "but nothing unusual."

Determined, Elias consulted the village elders, who shared hilarious tales of floating islands and nights when the stars rearranged themselves. One elder, however, warned him: "The scandal of your failure would reach the king himself." Undeterred, Elias began sketching closely, noting every tremor, every whisper of wind that seemed to register as a warning. By the third week, he had a theory: Fluxis wasn’t just shifting—it was alive.

His final map, a masterpiece of ink and instinct, was stolen the night before presentation. The thief? The same gang that had "protected" the land. But as Elias stood on the docks, watching his work sail away, he grinned. The map wasn’t just a guide—it was a bullet in a war between cartographers and the continent itself. And he intended to win.

Explanation: The text states the governor’s telegram explicitly mentions Elias’s maps as the only ones that keep up with Fluxis’s shifts. The other options are unsupported by the text—no political disputes, ocean maps, or treasures are mentioned.
---
