Correct: Because the king would hold Elias accountable if his mapping efforts failed to produce useful results.
Distractors: Because Elias had previously spread false rumors about Fluxis / Because the elders feared Elias would expose their supernatural beliefs / Because the king had already punished cartographers who mapped Fluxis incorrectly
Explanation: The elder’s warning ("The scandal of your failure would reach the king himself") implies political consequences, not personal attacks or prior misdeeds by Elias. The text does not suggest Elias had spread rumors or that the king had punished others—only that failure would have serious repercussions.
---
Q3: How did Elias react to the gang’s theft of his final map?
Correct: He saw it as a sign that his work was part of a larger conflict.
Distractors: He immediately reported the theft to the governor / He destroyed his notes in frustration / He tried to negotiate with the gang for its return
Explanation: The text describes Elias’s reaction as a grin and frames the theft as a "bullet in a war," implying he viewed it strategically, not as a setback. There’s no mention of reporting the theft, destroying notes, or negotiating.

Correct: That the continent was not just shifting randomly but was somehow sentient or alive.
Distractors: That the shifts were caused by underground volcanic activity / That the land was cursed by an ancient civilization / That the governor was exaggerating the changes for political gain
Explanation: Elias concludes that Fluxis "wasn’t just shifting—it was alive," directly contradicting natural explanations (volcanoes) or supernatural ones (curses). The text also dismisses the governor’s motives as desperate, not deceptive.

Explanation: The text emphasizes Elias’s methodical approach ("sketching closely," observing tremors and winds), not recklessness or dismissal of local knowledge. The other options are incorrect because the text shows he consulted elders, faced the gang’s threats, and did not work alone.



Topic: a fantasy story where the main character realises that their world is an illusion

Text:
Lena had always lived in the quiet village of Eldermere, where life followed a predictable routine. Every morning, she woke to the same golden sunrise, walked the same cobblestone paths, and served the same customers in her family’s bakery. The villagers spoke of the world beyond the misty hills, but no one ever returned from those journeys. Lena never questioned it—until the day she found the old lottery ticket in her grandmother’s attic.

The ticket was unlike any she’d seen before. Instead of numbers, it bore strange symbols that seemed to shift when she blinked. That night, she dreamed of a vast, shimmering library where books folded into themselves like origami. A voice whispered, "You were never meant to stay." When she woke, her hands trembled. She decided to test fate: she’d walk forward past the village’s edge, where the mist dominated the horizon.

At first, nothing changed. Then, the trees began to flicker like candle flames. A gust of wind carried a scent she’d never smelled before—ozone and something electric. Lena’s breath hitched as the world around her stuttered, like a film reel skipping. She realized, with a sickening clarity, that Eldermere was an illusion. The villagers were puppets, their agency stolen by unseen forces. The lottery ticket wasn’t a game; it was a key. But as she turned to run, a shadow arose from the mist—a figure with hollow eyes, its smile too wide, too random. "You shouldn’t have looked," it hissed. Lena’s legs refused to move. The bakery’s bell chimed behind her, and the scent of fresh bread filled the air. The mist swallowed the figure whole.

When Lena returned to her routine, her grandmother smiled and handed her a tray of pastries. "You’re late," she said, as if nothing had happened. Lena’s fingers dug into her palm. The lottery ticket was gone. But in the attic, a single symbol from the ticket now glowed faintly on her wrist—unacceptable proof that she had seen the truth.

Distractors: A literal place villagers fear to visit / A metaphor for death / A hidden treasure the villagers seek.

The correct answer fits because the text describes the misty hills as a boundary no one crosses, and Lena’s journey beyond them reveals the truth about her world. The other options are incorrect: villagers don’t fear the hills (they’re just ignored), there’s no mention of treasure, and "death" is never implied—only the illusion’s collapse.
---

Distractors: It’s the only ticket with numbers / It’s torn and damaged / It’s from a different country.
Explanation:
The correct answer aligns with the text’s detail about the symbols shifting when Lena blinks, suggesting supernatural qualities. The distractors are wrong because the ticket has no numbers, isn’t described as damaged, and there’s no mention of its origin.
---
Q3: What does the figure with "hollow eyes" represent?
Correct: A manifestation of the illusion’s control mechanism punishing Lena for questioning it.
Distractors: A lost villager trapped in the illusion / A guardian protecting Eldermere / A hallucination caused by hunger.
Explanation:
The figure’s appearance and warning ("You shouldn’t have looked") imply it’s a force enforcing the illusion’s rules. The distractors fail because the text never suggests the figure is a villager, guardian, or hallucination—it’s clearly tied to the illusion’s collapse.
---
Q4: How does Lena’s grandmother’s reaction ("You’re late") reinforce the theme of the story?
Correct: It proves the illusion resets to erase Lena’s awareness of the truth.
Distractors: It shows Lena is forgetful / It highlights the grandmother’s cruelty / It suggests the grandmother is also an illusion.
Explanation:
The correct answer fits because the grandmother’s line ignores Lena’s entire experience, implying the illusion "rewinds" her memory. The distractors are wrong: Lena isn’t forgetful (she’s traumatized), the grandmother isn’t cruel, and while she is part of the illusion, the text doesn’t focus on her as a separate entity—just as a tool to reset reality.
---
Q5: What does the "single symbol" on Lena’s wrist symbolize?
Correct: Unerasable proof that she briefly escaped the illusion’s control.
Distractors: A curse marking her as different / A sign she won the lottery / Evidence of a skin condition.
Explanation:
The correct answer matches the text’s emphasis on the symbol as "unacceptable proof"—it’s the only remnant of her realization. The distractors are wrong: there’s no lottery win, no curse mentioned, and the symbol’s glow suggests magic, not a medical issue.



Topic: a fantasy story about a city built on the back of a slowly moving, colossal creature

Text:

The city’s domestic life was orderly, yet its existence was a challenge unlike any other. The severe winds that howled across Titanis’ ridges demanded sturdy homes, and the creature’s occasional shifts forced residents to relocate entire districts overnight.

Spinehold’s founders had once purchased a rare certificate from the Oracle of Echoes, granting them the right to occupy Titanis’ back. The document emphasized the city’s duty to honor the beast’s soul, for Titanis was more than a mount—it was a metaphor for endurance, a living testament to the balance between civilization and nature. The following generations, however, grew complacent. They forgot the warnings of their ancestors and built taller, heavier structures, straining the creature’s patience.

Now, as the city’s weight grew, Titanis’ movements grew erratic. The elders whispered of a reckoning, but the merchants and scholars dismissed them as superstitious. After all, hadn’t the city always survived? Yet, deep in the soul of Spinehold, a quiet fear lingered—one that even the grandest spires could not silence.

Topic: a suspenseful story about a portal to a dimension where thoughts become reality

Text:
The old library in Blackwood Hollow was known for its exotic collection of forbidden books, but none were as dangerous as the one Dr. Elias Voss had found. Its leather cover was cracked, and the title—"The Doorway of Whispers"—was barely legible. Inside, a passage described a hidden portal beneath the floorboards, one that could transport a traveler to a dimension where thoughts became reality. Skeptical but risky, Elias decided to test it.

The night he activated the mechanism, the floorboards groaned, and a swirling vortex appeared. Before he could hesitate, a strange worm-like creature slithered out, its many eyes instantly locking onto him. Elias stumbled back, his elbow scraping the stone wall. The creature hissed, "You dare enter?" Elias, though unfortunate in his curiosity, had no choice but to step forward. The moment he crossed the threshold, the world dissolved into a shifting landscape of his own fears—towering shadows, voices accusing him of secrets he’d never spoken.

Worse still, the dimension seemed to precede his thoughts. A contest began: every fear he harbored manifested around him, growing more vivid. Elias realized too late that the portal didn’t just reflect his mind—it fed on it. As he fought to escape, the creature’s laughter echoed, "Now you’ll never leave."

Questions:
Q1: What made Dr. Elias Voss decide to test the portal despite his skepticism?
Correct: Its reputation as the most dangerous book in the library
Distractors: The promise of exotic treasures hidden beneath the floor / A direct challenge from the library’s curator / A bet he couldn’t refuse from a colleague
Explanation: The text states the book was "forbidden" and "dangerous," which aligns with its reputation as the most perilous item. The other options are unsupported—there’s no mention of treasures, a curator’s challenge, or a bet. The risky nature of the book (implied by its danger) is the key motivator.
---
Q2: How did the worm-like creature react when Elias activated the portal?
Correct: It slithered out and locked its eyes onto him
Distractors: It dissolved into the vortex immediately / It accused Elias of trespassing / It offered to guide him through the portal
Explanation: The creature’s action is described as "slithered out" and its eyes "instantly locked" onto Elias. The other options contradict the text: it didn’t dissolve, accuse, or offer help—it was hostile and observant.
---
Q3: What happened to Elias’s fears in the dimension?
Correct: They manifested as physical threats around him
Distractors: They faded into harmless illusions / They were projected as a contest for others to solve / They became part of the worm’s body
Explanation: The text explains the dimension "dissolved into a shifting landscape of his own fears" and that they "manifested" as threats. The other options are incorrect: fears didn’t fade, weren’t a contest for others, or merge with the worm.
---
Q4: Why was Elias’s situation in the dimension described as a "contest"?
Correct: Because his fears grew stronger the more he resisted them
Distractors: Because the worm challenged him to a game of wits / Because he had to outrun his own thoughts / Because the portal required a verbal contest to exit
Explanation: The text states the dimension "seemed to precede his thoughts" and his fears "manifested... growing more vivid," implying a competitive dynamic between his mind and the environment. The other options introduce unsupported elements (a game, verbal contest, or outrunning thoughts).
---
Q5: What did the worm’s final words imply about Elias’s fate?
Correct: He would be trapped in the dimension forever
Distractors: He would be transformed into a worm-like creature / He would win the contest if he confessed his fears / He would be judged by an unseen jury
Explanation: The worm’s line—"Now you’ll never leave"—directly suggests permanent entrapment. The other options are contradicted: no transformation, confession, or jury is mentioned. The unfortunate outcome is his inability to escape.



Topic: an essay on the societal impact of the four-day work week

Text:
The idea of a four-day work week has gained relevant attention in recent years as companies attempt to balance productivity and employee well-being. While some argue that reducing work hours could harm economic growth, others believe it could transform society for the better. One neutral observer might point to historical examples, such as the canal workers of the 19th century, who often labored under harsh conditions. Today, advocates suggest that shorter workweeks could improve nutrition, mental health, and work-life balance.

Critics, however, question whether employees will fulfil their responsibilities with fewer hours. Some fear that businesses might beg for government subsidies to sustain operations. Yet, pilot programs in countries like Iceland and Japan show promising results, with workers reporting higher job satisfaction and even increased productivity. The signature of success may lie in flexible scheduling, allowing employees to trade traditional hours for more personal time.

The debate remains complex, but one thing is clear: the subject of workweek reform is no longer fringe. As societies evolve, the four-day week could redefine not just labor but also social dynamics, from family time to community engagement.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest could improve due to a four-day work week?
Correct: Nutrition
Distractors: Housing standards / Transportation efficiency / Government funding
Explanation: The text explicitly mentions "nutrition" as one of the potential benefits of a shorter workweek, linking it to improved well-being. The other options are unrelated—housing, transportation, and government funding are not discussed in this context.
---
Q2: Which historical example does the text reference to illustrate harsh working conditions?
Correct: Canal workers of the 19th century
Distractors: Factory workers during the Industrial Revolution / Agricultural laborers in the 18th century / Mining crews in the 20th century
Explanation: The text directly compares modern workweek debates to "canal workers of the 19th century", emphasizing their grueling conditions. The other options are plausible but not mentioned in the passage.
---
Q3: What do pilot programs in Iceland and Japan demonstrate about the four-day work week?
Correct: Higher job satisfaction and increased productivity
Distractors: Lower employee turnover / Reduced need for government intervention / Decreased company profits
Explanation: The text states that these programs show "higher job satisfaction and even increased productivity", making this the correct answer. The other options are either unsupported or contradicted by the text.
---
Q4: Why might businesses struggle financially if the four-day work week is adopted?
Correct: They might beg for government subsidies
Distractors: Workers could demand higher salaries / Productivity would decline sharply / Employees would refuse to trade hours for leisure
Explanation: The text warns that businesses "might beg for government subsidies" to sustain operations, implying financial strain. The other options are speculative or unsupported by the passage.
---
Q5: What does the text imply about the current debate on workweek reform?
Correct: It is no longer considered a fringe or extreme idea
Distractors: It is primarily supported by governments / It has been proven ineffective globally / It is only relevant to developed nations
Explanation: The text states that the subject of workweek reform is "no longer fringe", meaning it has gained mainstream acceptance. The other options are either incorrect or not mentioned.



Topic: a story about a world where a person's life expectancy is visible above their head

Text:
In the futuristic city of Lumina, a radical social experiment had taken root: every citizen’s life expectancy was displayed as a floating number above their head. The idea, proposed by a promising young scientist named Elara Voss, aimed to encourage healthier lifestyles and accompany policy changes to reduce preventable deaths. At first, the concept gained popularity among health advocates, who argued it would create urgency around wellness. However, as the numbers became visible, public reactions shifted dramatically.

Protests erupted when wealthy elites, whose numbers hovered around 90+, flaunted their longevity, while the poor—often marked with figures below 60—faced sincere outrage. Critics accused the system of deepening inequality, especially during the recent recession, when medical expenses skyrocketed. Elara, though sincere in her intentions, struggled to defend the project when data revealed the entire system disproportionately favored the rich. A citizens’ fund was later established to subsidize healthcare, but the damage to trust remained. The experiment highlighted how visibility—even with good intentions—could distort priorities, placing undue emphasis on superficial metrics over systemic change.

Questions:
Q1: What was the primary goal of Elara Voss’s life expectancy display system?
Correct: To encourage healthier lifestyles and support policy changes
Distractors: To punish people with low life expectancy / To increase medical expenses / To track criminal records
Explanation:
The text states the system was designed to "encourage healthier lifestyles and accompany policy changes." The other options are incorrect because the system was not punitive (distractor 1), did not aim to increase expenses (distractor 2), and had nothing to do with criminal tracking (distractor 3).
---
Q2: Why did the life expectancy display system face protests?
Correct: Because it highlighted inequality between rich and poor citizens
Distractors: Because it was too expensive to maintain / Because it violated privacy laws / Because it only showed numbers for criminals
Explanation:
The protests occurred due to visible disparities: "wealthy elites... flaunted their longevity, while the poor... faced outrage." The other options are incorrect because the text does not mention cost as a protest issue (distractor 1), privacy violations (distractor 2), or criminal targeting (distractor 3).
---
Q3: What was the outcome of the public backlash against the system?
Correct: A citizens’ fund was created to subsidize healthcare
Distractors: The system was immediately shut down / Elara resigned from her position / The government banned all health-related data
Explanation:
The text specifies that a "citizens’ fund was later established to subsidize healthcare," not that the system was shut down (distractor 1), Elara resigned (distractor 2), or health data was banned (distractor 3).
---
Q4: How did the recession affect the life expectancy display system?
Correct: It worsened the system’s negative impact by increasing medical expenses
Distractors: It led to the system being temporarily hidden / It caused the government to fund the project / It made the numbers more accurate
Explanation:
The text explains that during the recession, "medical expenses skyrocketed," exacerbating inequality. The other options are incorrect because the system was not hidden (distractor 1), the government did not fund it further (distractor 2), and accuracy was not the issue (distractor 3).
---
Q5: What did Elara Voss struggle to defend after the system’s flaws became clear?
Correct: The system’s fairness and lack of bias toward the rich
Distractors: The system’s scientific accuracy / The system’s popularity among politicians / The system’s ability to predict exact death dates
Explanation:
The text notes Elara struggled to defend the project when "data revealed the entire system disproportionately favored the rich." The other options are incorrect because the focus was on fairness, not scientific accuracy (distractor 1), political popularity (distractor 2), or exact predictions (distractor 3).



Topic: a reflective essay on the concept of home in an increasingly globalized and mobile society

Text:
The concept of home has evolved considerably in our globalized world, where borders blur and identities shift like sand. For many, home is no longer just a physical space but a bond—a feeling tied to memories, people, and even the scent of rain on a golden afternoon. Yet, in an era of constant movement, what truly defines home? For early settlers, it was land and survival; today, it is often flexibility and connection.

The apparent stability of home is challenged by economic shifts, such as the 2008 recession, which forced families to relocate or redefine their routines. A friend once told me that her sense of home followed her—a marker not of walls, but of shared laughter over coffee. Still, some cling to tradition, seeing home as a fixed place, while others embrace fluidity, carrying their cheek (and culture) with them wherever they go. Thus, home is both anchor and voyage, a paradox shaped by our mobile lives.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author suggest is the most significant change in the modern concept of home?
Correct: It is increasingly tied to emotional connections rather than just physical space.
Distractors: It has become less important due to globalization / It is now defined solely by economic stability / It is only relevant to older generations
Explanation: The text emphasizes that home is now a bond (emotional connection) and not just a physical place, as seen in phrases like "a feeling tied to memories" and "shared laughter." The other options contradict the text’s focus on emotional and flexible definitions of home, not its decline or economic ties.
---
Q2: How does the author describe the impact of the 2008 recession on the idea of home?
Correct: It forced families to redefine their routines and sometimes relocate.
Distractors: It made home more stable for most people / It led to a universal rejection of modern housing / It proved that home was irrelevant to economic crises
Explanation: The text states the recession "forced families to relocate or redefine their routines," directly linking economic instability to changes in home. The distractors misrepresent the text’s tone (stability, rejection, irrelevance) or lack evidence.
---
Q3: What does the author imply about the golden afternoon mentioned in the text?
Correct: It symbolizes nostalgic, cherished memories associated with home.
Distractors: It represents a literal golden hour of sunlight / It is a metaphor for economic prosperity / It highlights the physical beauty of a house
Explanation: The phrase "golden afternoon" is paired with "memories, people, and... scent of rain," suggesting nostalgia and emotional value. The distractors ignore the contextual clues (nostalgia, bond) and focus on literal or unrelated meanings.
---
Q4: Why does the author mention settlers in the paragraph?
Correct: To contrast historical definitions of home (land/survival) with modern, flexible ones.
Distractors: To argue that settlers had the best understanding of home / To criticize settlers for their rigid views / To suggest that modern people are worse at defining home
Explanation: The text uses settlers as a historical marker to show how home’s meaning has shifted from fixed land to emotional/fluid connections. The distractors misrepresent the author’s neutral, comparative tone and imply judgment, which isn’t present.
---
Q5: What does the phrase "carrying their cheek (and culture) with them" most likely mean?
Correct: Maintaining cultural identity and warmth despite physical movement.
Distractors: Literally carrying facial expressions as cultural artifacts / Rejecting cultural traditions in favor of mobility / Using humor (cheek) to cope with displacement
Explanation: "Cheek" here is idiomatic (e.g., "a cheeky smile"), representing warmth and cultural pride. The text pairs it with "culture" and mobility, implying identity preservation. The distractors treat it literally or ignore the cultural context.



Topic: an article exploring the ethics of digital legacies and social media profiles after death

Text:
In the digital age, the question of what happens to our online presence after death has become broadly discussed. Many people possess social media accounts filled with memories, messages, and even unfinished thoughts. The idea of a "digital legacy" raises ethical dilemmas: Should these accounts be deleted, preserved, or passed on to loved ones? Some argue that leaving behind a reasonably organized profile can be a wise way to honor a person’s life, especially if it contains meaningful content. However, others worry that overcoming the emotional toll of managing a deceased mate’s account may be difficult.

The debate also touches on environmental concerns. Storing data consumes energy, contributing to emission levels—though this is often overshadowed by the emotional weight of the decision. Some platforms, like Facebook’s "Memorialized Accounts," allow families to lock profiles, preventing further posts while keeping memories intact. Yet, legal and martial (civil) disputes can arise over who has the right to control these accounts. For example, a sibling might argue they should manage a profile, while a partner claims ownership. The lack of clear guidelines makes these situations complex.

Critics also point out that some platforms encourage users to preserve accounts indefinitely, which may not always align with the deceased’s wishes. For instance, a person might have wanted their posts erased, but their acid-tongued (sharp) final message could spark conflict if left visible. Ultimately, the ethics of digital legacies depend on balancing respect for the deceased, emotional well-being, and practical considerations like data storage and legal rights.

Questions:
Q1: What is one environmental concern related to preserving digital legacies?
Correct: emission
Distractors: acid / martial / mate
Explanation:
- Correct: The text mentions that storing data consumes energy, contributing to emission levels (e.g., carbon emissions).
- Distractors:
  - acid refers to a sharp or critical tone in writing, not environmental impact.
  - martial relates to civil/legal disputes, not environmental issues.
  - mate is used to describe a partner, irrelevant to emissions.
---
Q2: Why might managing a deceased person’s social media account be emotionally challenging?
Correct: overcoming the emotional toll
Distractors: possessing meaningful content / broadly discussing ethics / wise decisions
Explanation:
- Correct: The text states that overcoming the emotional difficulty of managing a deceased mate’s account can be hard.
- Distractors:
  - possessing meaningful content describes the account’s value, not the emotional burden.
  - broadly discussing ethics refers to the debate itself, not personal struggle.
  - wise decisions suggests a positive outcome, not a challenge.
---
Q3: What does the text imply about Facebook’s "Memorialized Accounts" feature?
Correct: It locks profiles to prevent new posts while keeping memories visible.
Distractors: It deletes all accounts automatically / It requires legal approval to access / It charges a fee for preservation
Explanation:
- Correct: The text explains that Memorialized Accounts are locked to prevent further posts but retain memories.
- Distractors:
  - deletes all accounts automatically is false; the feature preserves content.
  - requires legal approval is not mentioned; access is typically granted to close contacts.
  - charges a fee is unsupported by the text.
---
Q4: How does the text describe the tone of a deceased person’s final message if left visible?
Correct: acid-tongued
Distractors: martial / broadly critical / wise
Explanation:
- Correct: The text uses acid-tongued (sharp/critical) to describe a potentially conflict-causing final message.
- Distractors:
  - martial refers to legal disputes, not tone.
  - broadly critical is vague and not the exact phrasing used.
  - wise implies thoughtful, the opposite of sharp criticism.
---
Q5: What legal issue arises from disputes over controlling a deceased person’s social media?
Correct: Civil (martial) disputes
Distractors: Environmental regulations / Data emission laws / Possession rights
Explanation:
- Correct: The text mentions martial (civil) disputes over who controls the account.
- Distractors:
  - environmental regulations are unrelated to access rights.
  - data emission laws are not discussed as a legal conflict.
  - possession rights is partially correct but less precise than "civil disputes."



Topic: a reflective essay on the value of solitude in a hyper-connected world

Text:
In a world where smartphones buzz with notifications and social media coverage dominates our attention, the idea of solitude often feels like a relic of the past. Yet, as a creature of habit, I’ve come to recognize its quiet power. While technology offers unparalleled convenience—connecting us instantly to friends, news, and entertainment—it also fragments our focus, turning us into passive operators of digital algorithms. An analyst might argue that solitude is inefficient, but I’ve found it sharpens my mind, much like a firm grip on a pencil refines an artist’s output.

Society often glorifies constant activity, framing solitude as laziness or withdrawal. Yet, history’s greatest thinkers—from Einstein to Virginia Woolf—thrived in isolation. The pursuit of connection, while rewarding, can leave us exhausted, our thoughts scattered like leaves in a storm. Solitude, by contrast, is a sanctuary where ideas coalesce, unfiltered by the noise of others. It’s not about escaping people but about reclaiming space to think deeply, to question, and to grow.

Questions:
Q1: What does the author suggest about the relationship between technology and human focus?
Correct: Technology fragments human focus by making us passive operators of digital algorithms.
Distractors: Technology enhances focus by providing structured tasks / Technology eliminates the need for solitude / Technology replaces the role of human operators
Explanation: The text explicitly states that technology "fragments our focus" and turns people into "passive operators of digital algorithms." The distractors contradict this by implying positive or neutral effects (enhancing focus, eliminating solitude) or misrepresent the role of technology (replacing operators), which the text does not support.
---
Q2: How does the author compare solitude to the pursuit of constant connection?
Correct: Solitude is a sanctuary for deep thought, while constant connection leaves people exhausted.
Distractors: Solitude is a form of withdrawal from society / Constant connection is more efficient than solitude / Solitude prevents people from thinking deeply
Explanation: The author contrasts solitude as a "sanctuary where ideas coalesce" with the exhaustion caused by the pursuit of connection. The distractors misrepresent solitude as purely negative (withdrawal), overstate efficiency (which the text critiques), or falsely claim solitude hinders thought (the opposite is argued).
---
Q3: What does the author imply about the output of solitude compared to constant digital engagement?
Correct: Solitude produces sharper, more refined ideas than constant digital engagement.
Distractors: Solitude reduces creative output / Digital engagement yields more original ideas / Solitude and engagement produce equal results
Explanation: The text uses the metaphor of a "firm grip on a pencil" to suggest solitude refines output, implying higher quality. The distractors contradict this by claiming solitude diminishes creativity, overvalues digital engagement, or suggests equivalence, which the text rejects.
---
Q4: Why does the author mention historical figures like Einstein and Virginia Woolf?
Correct: To illustrate that solitude has historically been essential for deep thinking and creativity.
Distractors: To argue that solitude is outdated in modern society / To criticize their lack of social connections / To compare their productivity to modern digital users
Explanation: The author uses these figures to support the idea that solitude fosters creativity, not to dismiss it. The distractors misrepresent the purpose: the text does not claim solitude is outdated, criticize their connections, or compare productivity metrics.
---
Q5: What does the text suggest about the coverage provided by social media?
Correct: It dominates attention but often leaves people mentally scattered.
Distractors: It improves mental clarity and focus / It replaces the need for solitude / It ensures balanced emotional well-being
Explanation: The text describes social media coverage as overwhelming ("dominates our attention") and linked to scattered thoughts. The distractors present it as beneficial (improving clarity, replacing solitude) or universally positive (ensuring well-being), which contradicts the critical tone of the passage.



Topic: an article about the paradox of choice - why more options often make us less happy

Text:
The modern world offers an overwhelming volume of choices—from smartphones with countless logo designs to job opportunities spanning continents. Yet, research suggests that more options often lead to less satisfaction, a phenomenon psychologists call the paradox of choice. Why does this happen?

In rural areas, where traditions resist change, people often feel less stressed by limited options. A farmer might chop wood for heating without agonizing over brands or models. In contrast, urban dwellers face endless circuit-board-like menus of life decisions, from streaming services to immigration policies. Each choice demands mental energy, leaving us exhausted.

Consider a simple affair: picking a restaurant. A decade ago, locals might draft a shortlist of three nearby spots. Today, apps list hundreds, each with user reviews, dietary filters, and delivery times. The brain, overwhelmed, struggles to evaluate fairly. Studies show that when given 30 jam flavors, shoppers were happier than those faced with 24—despite the latter having more options.

The issue isn’t the choices themselves but the illusion of control. When options multiply, we fear missing out (FOMO) and second-guess every decision. Ironically, the freedom to choose more often traps us in a cycle of regret and dissatisfaction.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest is the main cause of the paradox of choice?
Correct: Overwhelming mental energy required to evaluate too many options.
Distractors: The lack of traditional values in urban areas / The physical exhaustion from making decisions / The influence of social media trends.
Explanation:
The text explains that the paradox of choice arises because the brain struggles to process and compare too many options, leading to mental fatigue (e.g., "Each choice demands mental energy"). The distractors are incorrect because:
- Traditional values are mentioned as a contrast (rural areas resist change) but aren’t the cause of the paradox.
- Physical exhaustion isn’t referenced; the focus is on cognitive overload.
- Social media trends aren’t discussed as a factor in the paradox.
---
Q2: How does the text compare decision-making in rural vs. urban areas?
Correct: Rural areas have fewer options, which reduces stress, while urban areas offer too many, causing anxiety.
Distractors: Urban areas make better decisions due to more information / Rural areas lack the skills to evaluate choices / Urban choices are simpler because of technology.
Explanation:
The text states rural areas "feel less stressed by limited options" (e.g., farmers chop wood without deliberating brands), while urban areas face "endless... menus of life decisions." The distractors fail because:
- Urban areas don’t make "better" decisions—they’re overwhelmed.
- Rural areas aren’t described as lacking skills—they’re simply less burdened by choice.
- Urban choices aren’t simpler—they’re more complex (e.g., apps with filters).
---
Q3: What does the jam-flavor study illustrate about choice?
Correct: More options can reduce satisfaction because they increase indecision and regret.
Distractors: People prefer jams with familiar logos / Fewer options lead to poorer quality choices / The study proves that urban jams taste better.
Explanation:
The study shows shoppers were happier with 30 flavors than 24, despite the latter having more options, due to "regret and dissatisfaction" from overchoice. The distractors are wrong because:
- Logos aren’t mentioned in the study.
- Quality isn’t the issue—it’s the volume of choices.
- Urban jams aren’t compared; the focus is on the number of options.
---
Q4: Which word from the text best describes the mental process of evaluating too many choices?
Correct: Circuit
Distractors: Volume / Logo / Chop
Explanation:
The text uses "circuit-like menus" to describe the overwhelming, interconnected complexity of choices. The other words don’t fit:
- Volume refers to quantity, not the process of evaluation.
- Logo is about branding, not decision-making.
- Chop is a physical action, unrelated to mental evaluation.
---
Q5: Why does the text mention immigration as an example of modern choices?
Correct: To show that complex, high-stakes decisions (like policies) add to the mental burden of choice.
Distractors: To argue that immigration is the most stressful choice / To compare rural and urban immigration rates / To suggest that logos on immigration forms cause confusion.
Explanation:
Immigration is cited as a "life decision" alongside other modern choices (e.g., streaming services) to highlight how complex options multiply stress. The distractors are incorrect because:
- The text doesn’t claim immigration is the most stressful—it’s one of many overwhelming choices.
- Rural vs. urban immigration rates aren’t discussed.
- Logos on forms aren’t mentioned as a factor.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of remote work

Text:
The rise of remote work has transformed how many professionals approach their careers, offering both opportunities and challenges. One of its key advantages is flexibility—employees can design their schedules around personal needs, balancing work with family or hobbies. This autonomy often motivates productivity, as people feel trusted to manage their time efficiently. Additionally, remote work eliminates commutes, saving time and money while reducing stress. Companies also benefit from a broader talent pool, as they can hire skilled workers regardless of origin or location, fostering diversity.

However, remote work isn’t without drawbacks. Without physical coverage in an office, some teams struggle with communication gaps, leading to misunderstandings or missed deadlines. The lack of face-to-face interaction can also ruin team morale, especially for those who thrive on collaboration. Moreover, not all roles suit remote settings—jobs requiring hands-on delivery, like construction or lab work, demand physical presence. Even in office-based fields, employees might feel isolated, lacking the cue of office chatter or spontaneous brainstorming sessions that spark creativity.

Some argue that remote work is ideal for freelancers or those who specialize in digital skills, such as graphic design or lyric writing. Yet, even in these cases, discipline is crucial. The line (lane) between work and personal life can blur, making it harder to disconnect. Ultimately, the success of remote work depends on clear expectations, strong digital tools, and a culture that values both independence and connection.

Questions:
Q1: What is one way remote work can negatively affect team dynamics?
Correct: ruin morale
Distractors: increase delivery times / reduce specialization / limit lyric creativity
Explanation:
- "ruin morale" fits because the text states that lack of interaction can harm team morale.
- "increase delivery times" is incorrect—remote work often speeds up delivery by cutting commutes, not slowing it.
- "reduce specialization" is wrong—remote work can expand opportunities for specialists, not limit them.
- "limit lyric creativity" is irrelevant; the text doesn’t link remote work to creative writing constraints.
---
Q2: Which phrase from the text suggests that remote work allows hiring without geographic restrictions?
Correct: regardless of origin
Distractors: broader talent pool / physical coverage / spontaneous brainstorming
Explanation:
- "regardless of origin" directly refers to hiring without location barriers.
- "broader talent pool" is plausible but vague—it doesn’t specify geographic flexibility.
- "physical coverage" implies presence, not hiring flexibility.
- "spontaneous brainstorming" relates to office interaction, not hiring.
---
Q3: What does the text imply about jobs that require hands-on tasks?
Correct: They may not suit remote work.
Distractors: They require lyric writing skills / They benefit from commutes / They need special digital tools
Explanation:
- The text explicitly mentions roles like construction or lab work "demand physical presence", making remote work unsuitable.
- "lyric writing skills" is unrelated—no connection is made between hands-on jobs and creative writing.
- "benefit from commutes" contradicts the text’s point that commutes are eliminated in remote work.
- "need special digital tools" is partially true but doesn’t address the core issue of physical requirements.
---
Q4: Which word in the text describes a signal or hint that helps communication in an office?
Correct: cue
Distractors: motivate / delivery / lane
Explanation:
- "cue" is used to describe office chatter or interactions that aid communication.
- "motivate" refers to productivity, not signals.
- "delivery" pertains to tasks or results, not communication hints.
- "lane" metaphorically describes work-life balance, not interaction signals.
---
Q5: What does the text suggest about freelancers and remote work?
Correct: It is ideal for those who specialize in digital skills.
Distractors: They struggle with discipline / They avoid lyric writing / They lack coverage
Explanation:
- The text states freelancers or digital specialists (e.g., graphic designers) "specialize" and thrive in remote work.
- "struggle with discipline" is a challenge mentioned but not exclusive to freelancers.
- "avoid lyric writing" is incorrect—lyric writing is given as an example of a remote-friendly skill.
- "lack coverage" is false; freelancers often choose remote work for its flexibility, not due to coverage issues.



Topic: a case study on the use of gamification in advertising

Text:
In the competitive world of advertising, brands are constantly searching for innovative ways to grab attention. One strategy gaining traction is gamification, where elements of game design are integrated into marketing campaigns to enhance engagement. A case study from a global beverage company illustrates this approach effectively. Facing declining sales, the company launched a mobile app where users could collect points for purchasing their products. These points could be exchanged for discounts or exclusive merchandise, acting as a powerful incentive. The campaign was a victory, with user participation surging by 40% within three months.

However, not all gamification efforts succeed. A poorly designed game-like promotion for a fast-food chain backfired spectacularly. The app promised rewards for completing challenges, but technical glitches and confusing rules led to frustration. Customers complained about harmful effects on their experience, comparing it to a bombing run of poor design. The company had to starve the project of funding mid-campaign, losing potential revenue.

Feedback from both cases highlights a key lesson: gamification works best when it aligns with user needs and provides clear, immediate rewards. The beverage company’s success came from saving customers time and effort while making engagement feel rewarding. In contrast, the fast-food chain’s failure showed how misaligned incentives and poor execution can turn a creative idea into a disaster.

Questions:
Q1: What was the primary reason the beverage company’s gamification campaign succeeded?
Correct: It provided clear incentives for user engagement.
Distractors: It relied on expensive technical features. / It avoided all digital elements. / It targeted only young adults.
Explanation: The text states the campaign used points for discounts and merchandise as incentives, directly driving participation. The other options are incorrect because the success was tied to rewards (not cost or exclusivity) and the campaign was digital (not avoiding tech) and not age-specific.
---
Q2: How did the fast-food chain’s gamification fail?
Correct: Technical glitches and confusing rules frustrated users.
Distractors: It lacked any form of rewards. / It was too similar to the beverage company’s model. / It received excessive funding.

Topic: a review of different architectural approaches to building in earthquake-prone zones

Text:
Building in earthquake-prone zones demands innovative architectural approaches, as traditional methods often fail to defend structures against seismic forces. Engineers and architects devote significant effort to studying past disasters, document their findings, and develop resilient designs. One widely cited strategy is the use of base isolators, which absorb shock waves before they reach the building. These systems are particularly appropriate in regions where earthquakes are frequent, such as Japan or California.

Another approach emphasizes diversity in construction materials. For instance, reinforced concrete and steel frames are favored for their flexibility, while lighter materials like wood can possess natural damping properties. However, wind resistance is also critical, as high-rise buildings in seismic zones must withstand both horizontal forces. Research institutes like the Pacific Earthquake Engineering Research Center conduct extensive enquirys to refine these techniques, ensuring safety without sacrificing aesthetics.

Critics argue that some methods prioritize cost over safety, but advancements in technology—such as smart sensors—now allow real-time monitoring. The key lies in balancing tradition with innovation, ensuring structures can endure nature’s fury while respecting cultural and environmental contexts.

Distractors: To increase the building’s height / To reduce wind resistance / To make structures more visually appealing
Explanation:
The text explicitly states that base isolators "absorb shock waves before they reach the building," making this the correct answer. The distractors are incorrect because:
- Increasing height or reducing wind resistance are unrelated to base isolators’ function.

For instance, the origin of many contemporary designs traces back to Scandinavian libraries, where minimalism and functionality are key. These spaces often feature open-plan layouts, collaborative zones, and short-term rental areas for events, allowing communities to engage actively.

However, maintaining such innovation requires careful planning. Libraries must balance revenue generation—through memberships or sponsorships—with their core mission of accessibility. Some institutions employ junior architects to design cost-effective solutions, while others collaborate with specialists like surgeons (yes, really!) to create healing spaces within medical libraries. The trend also reflects a cultural fever for experiential learning, with libraries incorporating VR labs, maker spaces, and even rooftop gardens.

Critics argue that not all designs are appropriately scaled for smaller towns, but proponents insist flexibility is the answer. By inferring user behavior—such as the rise of e-books—libraries redefine their purpose without losing their soul.

Clara realized her absence hadn’t just cost her skills; it had altered her status in ways she couldn’t reverse overnight. Clara, an observer of old techniques, noticed something unusual: faint brushstrokes beneath the visible layer. Intrigued, she convinced the local museum to let her examine it further.

Using advanced imaging technology, Clara revealed a hidden portrait—a woman holding a key. The museum director, skeptical at first, organized a contest to decode the image’s meaning. Teams competed to find clues about the woman’s identity, with prizes for the most positive and efficient solutions. Clara’s theory won: the key symbolized access to a lost treasure, buried where the bear in the painting stood.

During the completion of the contest, villagers shared stories of a bear statue near the old church—rumored to guard hidden wealth. Excavations confirmed Clara’s findings, but to her disappointment, the treasure was a collection of rare books, not gold. Still, the discovery sparked a debate on consumption of cultural heritage versus its preservation.

Distractors: The fastest excavation team / The most creative story / The largest donation to the museum.

Topic: a critical analysis of the wellness industry

The wellness industry has exploded in recent years, promising to heal both body and mind—but critics argue it often prioritizes profit over genuine well-being. Take, for example, the rise of "wellness retreats" in remote counties, where participants pay thousands for meditation in a zone of silence, only to find surgeons and therapists moonlighting as life coaches. The irony is palpable: while these retreats preserve the illusion of self-improvement, they rarely address systemic issues like healthcare access or workplace stress. Even the language used—terms like "healing journey" or "lyric" affirmations—can feel hollow when detached from real solutions.

Some spectators of this industry, including medical professionals, openly oppose its unregulated expansion. A 2023 study revealed that only 30% of wellness programs adequately follow evidence-based practices. Meanwhile, corporate wellness initiatives often reduce employees to productivity metrics, scheduling mandatory yoga sessions instead of tackling burnout. The industry’s growth reflects a cultural obsession with self-optimization, but at what cost? When wellness becomes a performative trend rather than a tool for collective healing, it risks becoming just another layer of consumerism.

Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of minimalism in interior design

Text:
Minimalism in interior design has become one of the most prime trends in recent years, offering both aesthetic and functional benefits. At its core, minimalism emphasizes simplicity, using numerous clean lines, neutral colors, and uncluttered spaces to create a sense of calm. One of its key advantages is its ability to reduce visual confusion, making rooms feel more organized and spacious. This approach is particularly effective in workplaces, where a tidy environment can boost productivity and focus. Additionally, minimalist designs often align with sustainable living, as they encourage the use of fewer, high-quality materials, reducing waste.

However, minimalism is not without its challenges. Critics argue that overly sparse designs can feel cold or entertaining—lacking warmth and personality. Without thoughtful decoration, a minimalist space may appear sterile or impersonal. Another drawback is the potential for diversity in perception; what one person finds serene, another might find boring. Historical examples, such as the bombing of minimalist art movements in the past, also highlight how easily such trends can be misinterpreted or rejected by the public. To succeed, designers must carefully balance minimalism with personal touches, gathering feedback from users to establish a space that truly resonates.

Questions:
Q1: What is one of the main goals of minimalism in interior design according to the text?
Correct: To reduce visual confusion
Distractors: To maximize color diversity / To create overly decorative spaces / To prioritize entertainment value
Explanation: The text states that minimalism aims to reduce visual confusion by using clean lines and uncluttered spaces. The other options contradict the core principles of minimalism (e.g., "color diversity" or "overly decorative") or are unrelated (e.g., "entertainment value").
---
Q2: Why might a minimalist workplace be beneficial?
Correct: It can boost productivity and focus
Distractors: It always feels warmer and more inviting / It requires more furniture to function / It is less sustainable than other designs
Explanation: The text explicitly links minimalist workplaces to improved productivity and focus. The distractors are incorrect because minimalism is often criticized for feeling cold ("warmer"), avoids clutter ("more furniture"), and is described as sustainable ("less sustainable" is false).

Q3: How does the text describe the potential downside of minimalist designs?
Correct: They can feel cold or lacking personality
Distractors: They are always confusing to decorate / They require constant feedback to maintain / They prioritize expensive materials over simplicity
Explanation: The text mentions that minimalism may lack warmth or personality, making it feel cold. The other options are unsupported: minimalism aims to reduce confusion, not create it; feedback is suggested but not mandatory; and while quality materials are used, the text doesn’t claim expense is the focus.

Correct: The bombing of minimalist art movements
Distractors: The rejection of maximalist trends in the 1980s / The decline of sustainable design in the 2000s / The failure of colorful interiors in the 1950s
Explanation: The text refers to "the bombing of minimalist art movements," implying criticism or backlash. The distractors introduce unrelated historical periods or trends not mentioned in the passage.

Distractors: Following strict color codes / Ignoring user feedback entirely / Using only vintage furniture
Explanation: The text emphasizes the need to add personal elements to avoid sterility, citing the importance of feedback to establish a resonant space. The distractors are incorrect because minimalism avoids rigid rules ("color codes"), requires user input ("ignoring feedback"), and doesn’t specify furniture styles ("vintage").

A recent study from a major urban county revealed that 68% of young adults admit to beating their friends by posting more "perfect" travel photos, even if the trips were mediocre.

The pressure to document every moment has led to an eruption of unrealistic expectations. Travelers now treat destinations like a database of Instagram-worthy moments, skipping authentic interactions for the sake of a well-framed shot. Critics, including travel referees like professional bloggers, warn that this culture dilutes the meaning of travel. What was once a journey of discovery has become a race for acceptable validation—where the pursuit of engagement overshadows the joy of the experience itself. The question remains: When does sharing become showing off, and when does it become a genuine connection with the world?

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break

Text:
The moment I realized I was perpetuating a cycle I had sworn to break came during a psychology seminar at university. Our chairman, a sophisticated professor with a sharp wit, presented a case study on generational trauma. He described how a troop of soldiers, once trained to follow orders blindly, later became terrorists—not because they were inherently violent, but because their leaders had conditioned them to see violence as the only solution.

I sat there, nodding along, until the discussion shifted to everyday life. A student raised her hand: "What about parents who yell at their kids the same way their parents yelled at them?" The room fell silent. I glanced at my cute notebook, where I’d scribbled "Break the cycle" in bold letters months ago. Whereas I had always seen myself as progressive, the truth hit me like a wave. My own impatient outbursts toward my little sister weren’t just frustration—they were echoes of my father’s temper. The concept of "love" I associated with discipline was, in reality, a chain I’d vowed to snap.

Subsequently, I spent weeks analyzing my behavior, reading books on attachment theory, and even journaling. The realization wasn’t just about me; it was about the invisible threads connecting generations. That seminar didn’t just teach me psychology—it forced me to confront the mirror.

Correct: A student’s question about parents yelling at their kids.
Distractors: The professor’s lecture on soldiers becoming terrorists / The narrator’s own impatient outbursts / A book on attachment theory
Explanation: The narrator’s epiphany was sparked by the student’s comment about generational yelling, not the professor’s broader example (which was background) or their own actions (which were the result of the realization). The book on attachment theory came after the moment of clarity.
---
Q2: How does the narrator describe their father’s behavior in relation to their own?
Correct: Echoes of my father’s temper.
Distractors: A direct imitation of his discipline / A conscious rebellion against his methods / A complete rejection of his parenting style
Explanation: The narrator uses the word "echoes" to emphasize unconscious repetition, not deliberate copying or rejection. "Discipline" was framed as part of the cycle, not a separate act.
---
Q3: What does the word "sophisticated" most likely describe about the professor?
Correct: His sharp wit and intellectual depth.
Distractors: His outdated teaching methods / His lack of empathy toward students / His reliance on simple examples
Explanation: The professor is portrayed as intellectually refined ("sharp wit") and nuanced, not rigid or simplistic. The context contrasts his sophisticated approach with the raw, cyclical behavior he critiques.
---
Q4: Why does the narrator mention the "cute" notebook?
Correct: To symbolize their naive self-perception before the realization.
Distractors: To highlight their artistic side / To show they were taking detailed notes / To contrast their childishness with the serious topic
Explanation: The notebook’s cuteness contrasts with the heavy theme, subtly revealing the narrator’s earlier blindness to their own behavior. It’s not about artistry or note-taking but about self-image.
---
Q5: What concept does the narrator ultimately associate with the "chain" they wanted to break?
Correct: Love tied to discipline through generational trauma.
Distractors: Freedom from parental control / The idea of unconditional acceptance / The necessity of strict rules
Explanation: The narrator links "love" to the cycle of trauma, not liberation or strictness. The "chain" represents inherited patterns, not a rejection of rules or a utopian ideal.



Topic: a story about mediating a conflict between two people I care about deeply

Text:
Last weekend, I found myself in the middle of a heated argument between two people I hold in the highest regard: my best friend, Liam, and my cousin, Mira. Their disagreement had been brewing for months—Liam, a conservative journalist, accused Mira, a nursing student, of being reckless with her unconscious patients. Mira, however, saw Liam’s criticism as hypocritical, given his own ethical blind spots. When I arrived, Liam was gesturing wildly, his elbow nearly knocking over a textbook on the coffee table.

I knew I had to act before the situation escalated. Taking a deep breath, I reminded myself that mediation wasn’t just compulsory—it was a moral duty. I started by acknowledging their emotions: "I can see how hurt and frustrated both of you are." Liam scoffed, calling Mira a "loose cannon," while Mira shot back, "At least I care about real people, not just headlines!" The tension was thick enough to cut with a knife.

I shifted the focus to their shared goal: improving patient care. "You’re both creatures of passion," I said, "but passion without precision is dangerous." Slowly, they began to listen. By the end, they agreed to meet weekly to discuss protocols—no more accusations, just solutions. The shock on their faces when they realized they’d been talking past each other for so long was priceless. Conflict, I learned, isn’t about winning; it’s about regard—for the truth, for each other, and for the greater good.

Q3: What metaphor does the narrator use to describe the tension between Liam and Mira?
Correct: "Thick enough to cut with a knife."
Distractors: "A storm about to burst" / "A silent, suffocating weight" / "A spark waiting to ignite"
Explanation: The text uses the knife metaphor to convey the palpable, almost physical tension. The other options are vivid but not mentioned in the passage.

Q4: How does the narrator attempt to de-escalate the situation initially?

Distractors: By imposing a time limit on their argument / By physically separating them / By quoting a conflict-resolution textbook

Topic: an essay on whether smartphones should be banned in schools

Text:
The debate over whether smartphones should be banned in schools has intensified as technology reshapes education. Proponents argue that these devices create an illusion of productivity, allowing students to transmit messages or browse social media instead of focusing on lessons. A recent study by an educational agency revealed that 60% of teachers believe smartphones disrupt classroom discussions, even when used discreetly. Critics, however, claim that banning them ignores the tools students need for modern learning, such as digital research or emergency communication.

Some schools have experimented with partial bans, requiring students to insert phones into lockers during classes. Yet, this approach has faced challenges, particularly in circumstances where students rely on their phones for transportation or insurance claims after a crash. Tech corporations like Apple and Samsung argue that smartphones can enhance education through apps for language learning or note-taking, while parents often worry about cyberbullying and screen addiction.

The chair of a local school board suggested a compromise: allowing phones only in designated areas, such as the library or cafeteria. However, opponents fear this could lead to loopholes, with students sneaking devices into classrooms. Ultimately, the question remains—should schools prioritize traditional learning environments or adapt to the digital age?

Leo, ever the optimist, saw their friendship as a flame that would never fade, while Mira, more pragmatic, believed in letting things take their natural course.

As they advanced in their careers, their paths diverged. Leo moved to the city to work in tech, while Mira stayed closer to home, hosting a regional food blog. Their conversations became less frequent, and replies grew delayed. One evening, after a year of silence, Leo sent a message: "We’re not fighting, are we?" Mira replied, "No, but we’re not the same people anymore." The literal truth was that life had pulled them apart—not anger, but the quiet erosion of shared time and dreams. Leo later called it a metaphor for how even the strongest bonds can drift, like two ships passing in the night, their elbows no longer brushing in the same café booth.

Topic: a personal story about the moment I realized I was jealous of a friend's success

Text:
The moment I realized I was jealous of my friend Mark’s success was during our university graduation ceremony. While I stood on the shore of the stage, watching him receive his diploma, I noticed something universal in my chest—a tight, uncomfortable nerve that refused to fade. Mark had always been brilliant, but this time, his achievement felt like a scheme designed to outshine me, even though we were equivalents in every way.

As the crowd applauded, I forced a smile, but inside, I felt an infection of bitterness. His promotion to a prestigious company, his status as the top student, and even his effortless participation in debates—everything seemed to beat me at my own game. That evening, I confessed my feelings to my sister, who called it "the evil of comparison." She was right. Jealousy had crept in like a thief, stealing my joy.

Now, I understand that success isn’t a zero-sum game, but that day taught me a hard lesson: envy thrives in silence. Mark’s triumph didn’t diminish me—it revealed my own unmet desires.

Topic: a story about how an unusual compromise resolved a conflict

Text:
The small village of Eldermere had long been divided between two rival families—the tech-savvy Hargroves, who dominated the town’s economy with their technological innovations, and the traditional Blackwoods, who suspected the Hargroves of exploiting the land in spite of their promises. Tensions reached a boiling point when the Hargroves announced plans to build a massive solar farm on the Blackwoods’ ancestral land, leaving the villagers in a depressing stalemate. The elders, however, proposed an unusual solution: a shared project that would reinforce both sides’ strengths.

The compromise involved creating a hybrid energy system—solar panels dominated by Blackwood expertise in sustainable farming, while Hargrove engineers optimized the technological infrastructure. The opening ceremony was held at dawn, where an unconscious (yet symbolic) gesture occurred: as the first beam of sunlight hit the panels, a Blackwood elder and a Hargrove engineer accidentally touched hands, breaking the silence. The crowd, moved by the moment, realized the wise decision had already begun to heal their divide. Hence, the project became a model for cooperation, proving that even the deepest conflicts could yield unexpected solutions when both sides let go of pride.

Questions:

Correct: The Hargroves’ plans to build a solar farm on Blackwood ancestral land.
Distractors: Their disagreement over traditional farming methods / A technological accident on Blackwood property / The Blackwoods’ refusal to adopt modern energy.
Explanation: The text states the conflict arose from the Hargroves’ land-use plans, not farming methods, accidents, or Blackwood resistance to technology. The other options misrepresent the cause (e.g., "traditional farming" is irrelevant; "accident" and "refusal" are unsupported).
---

Explanation: The elders’ solution was collaborative, not adversarial. The other options imply coercion, legal battles, or displacement—none of which are mentioned. The text emphasizes shared innovation.

Distractors: It forced the Blackwoods to adopt Hargrove innovations / It ignored the villagers’ depressing mood / It dominated one side’s interests over the other.
Explanation: The compromise was wise because it integrated both sides’ values (tech + tradition). The distractors imply coercion ("forced"), emotional neglect ("ignored mood"), or imbalance ("dominated"), which contradict the text’s emphasis on mutual benefit.

Explanation: The text highlights the lesson of letting go of pride ("unexpected solutions"). The distractors promote false universals (tech dominance, tradition over innovation) or distrust, which the story disproves. The correct answer aligns with the elders’ wise approach.



Topic: an essay about the advantages and disadvantages of developing tourism

Text:
Tourism has transformed from a formerly modest industry into one of the world’s most powerful economic forces, driven by the desire to explore new cultures and landscapes. While its growth brings golden opportunities, it also raises concerns about sustainability. Coastal towns, for example, now rely on visitors for income, but temporarily overcrowding strains local resources. Some argue that tourism improves infrastructure, creating jobs and boosting small businesses, making it an acceptable solution for economic struggles. However, critics point to environmental damage, such as pollution from cruise ships running on steam and diesel engines, which harms marine ecosystems.

Cultural preservation is another delicate balance. In some destinations, traditional practices are performed openly for tourists, risking commercialization. For instance, indigenous ceremonies may lose their sacred meaning when turned into entertainment. Meanwhile, the rating of hotels and attractions often prioritizes luxury over authenticity, leaving locals on the heel of progress. Even the smallest changes, like replacing handmade crafts with mass-produced tissue-thin souvenirs, reflect this shift. While tourism can unite people, its rapid expansion demands careful planning to ensure benefits outweigh the costs.

Topic: a case study of a company making a positive impact on the environment and society

Text:
In 2010, a tragedy struck the small island of Tala in the Pacific when a firework factory explosion destroyed local homes and polluted the ocean. The island’s tribe, already struggling with rising sea levels, faced another crisis. However, this disaster became the catalyst for change when founder Mara Nui, a local activist, decided to turn the tragedy into an opportunity. She launched Te Moana, a company that repurposes discarded fishing nets and plastic waste—collected wherever possible—into eco-friendly building materials.

Te Moana’s approach is stunning in its simplicity. Using traditional weaving techniques combined with modern recycling methods, the company transforms waste into durable, water-resistant panels. These panels are now used in schools, hospitals, and even temporary shelters for displaced communities. The input from local artisans ensures cultural heritage is preserved while creating jobs. The company also funds reforestation projects, planting mangroves to protect coastlines—a direct response to the time-worn threats of erosion.

Critics initially opposed the project, arguing that such innovations would take time to gain traction. Yet, within five years, Te Moana expanded to five Pacific islands, proving that sustainability can be both practical and profitable. Today, the company’s model inspires similar initiatives globally, showing that even the smallest communities can lead environmental change.

Topic: an essay on whether zoos should be banned

Text:
The debate over whether zoos should be banned is a complex subject that involves ethical, scientific, and psychological considerations. Proponents argue that zoos provide secure environments for endangered species, allowing skilled conservationists to monitor their health, nutrition, and breeding. For example, overseas zoos have successfully reintroduced animals like the Arabian oryx into the wild. However, critics claim that zoos exploit animals for entertainment, reducing them to mere monsters in cages. They argue that such conditions cause stress, leading to psychological harm.

Supporters counter that zoos motivate public interest in wildlife, funding conservation efforts. They also serve as educational tools, teaching visitors about biodiversity. Yet, opponents question whether zoos truly evaluate their impact fairly, ignoring the suffering of animals confined to artificial habitats. Some even compare zoos to modern-day mosques of exploitation, where profit overshadows ethics. The dilemma remains: can zoos balance conservation with compassion, or should they be abolished entirely?

Topic: an opinion essay on whether university education should be free for everyone

Text:
Should university education be free for everyone? The debate over this issue has grown louder in recent years, especially as the destruction of traditional job markets and the rising load of student debt threaten future generations. While some argue that free education would be a revolution in social equality, others believe it would strain public finances. However, the benefits of universal access to higher learning far outweigh the risks.

First, free education could rescue millions from financial ruin. Today, many students graduate with crippling debts, forcing them into low-paying jobs as clerks or service workers instead of pursuing their passions. By eliminating tuition fees, society could detect and nurture talent regardless of circumstance, ensuring that bright minds from poor backgrounds aren’t left behind. This isn’t just about fairness—it’s about unlocking human potential.

Critics claim free education is unrealistic, but history shows that bold reforms often begin as distant dreams before becoming reality. The latest economic models even suggest that educated populations drive innovation, reducing unemployment and boosting tax revenues. Free education isn’t just an ideal; it’s an investment in a stronger, more resilient society. Fundamentally, the question isn’t whether we can afford it, but whether we can afford not to.

Topic: an essay on the psychology of risk-taking: why some people seek danger and others avoid it

Text:
The psychology of risk-taking reveals fascinating differences in how people perceive danger. Some individuals are naturally drawn to thrill-seeking activities, while others avoid risks at all costs. Research suggests that personality traits, upbringing, and even biology play a role in shaping these behaviors.

From an evolutionary angle, risk-takers may have inherited traits that once ensured survival—such as boldness in hunting or exploration. However, modern society often discourages such behavior, labeling it reckless rather than adaptive. For example, a loyal friend might admire a skydiver’s courage but worry about their safety. Meanwhile, an opponent of extreme sports might argue that the risks outweigh the rewards, especially when considering potential disability or injury.

Neuroscientific studies show that risk-takers often experience a rush of dopamine, the "reward" chemical, when facing uncertainty. This chemical reaction can make danger feel exhilarating. Conversely, those who avoid risks may have a stronger fear response, triggered by the brain’s amygdala. Interestingly, even small risks—like breaking a wrist during a minor accident—can leave a lasting impression, shaping future behavior.

The completion of a high-stakes task, such as climbing a mountain or editing a controversial film, can bring immense recognition, reinforcing the habit of seeking challenges. However, not everyone craves this validation. Some prefer stability, fearing failure or social judgment. Understanding these motivations helps explain why people respond so differently to the same situations.

Questions:
Q1: What does the text suggest about the evolutionary origins of risk-taking behavior?
Correct: It may have ensured survival through traits like boldness in hunting or exploration.
Distractors: It is a modern phenomenon with no biological basis / It is solely influenced by cultural trends / It is always associated with recklessness.
Explanation: The text explicitly states that risk-taking traits (e.g., boldness) may have had survival benefits in evolutionary history (e.g., hunting). The distractors are incorrect because the text does not claim risk-taking is modern (eliminates option 1), does not attribute it solely to culture (eliminates option 2), and distinguishes recklessness from adaptive risk-taking (eliminates option 3).
---
Q2: How does the text describe the role of dopamine in risk-taking?
Correct: It makes danger feel exhilarating for risk-takers.
Distractors: It reduces fear responses in all individuals / It is absent in people who avoid risks / It only affects physical injuries like broken wrists.
Explanation: The text explains that dopamine triggers a "reward" sensation during uncertainty, making danger thrilling for risk-takers. The distractors are wrong because dopamine’s role isn’t universal (eliminates option 1), it’s not absent in risk-avoiders (eliminates option 2), and it’s not limited to physical injuries (eliminates option 3).
---
Q3: Why might a loyal friend admire a skydiver’s courage but still worry?
Correct: Because they recognize the courage but fear the potential for injury or disability.
Distractors: Because they disagree with the skydiver’s life choices / Because they believe skydiving lacks social recognition / Because they are the skydiver’s opponent in extreme sports.
Explanation: The text contrasts admiration for courage with concern for safety (e.g., disability). The distractors are incorrect because the text doesn’t mention disagreement with choices (eliminates option 1), social recognition isn’t the focus (eliminates option 2), and "opponent" refers to someone who opposes extreme sports, not a friend (eliminates option 3).
---
Q4: What does the text imply about the amygdala’s role in risk-avoidance?
Correct: It triggers a stronger fear response in people who avoid risks.
Distractors: It suppresses the desire for recognition in risk-takers / It is only active during physical accidents / It encourages people to seek high-stakes tasks.
Explanation: The text states the amygdala’s fear response is heightened in risk-avoiders. Too often, history is taught through the lens of conquerors and settlers, ignoring the deeper, more resilient tales of the people who first shaped the earth. This wisdom is not just folklore; it is a survival guide, immune to the frequent upheavals of human conflict.

Take the example of the ancient highway systems carved into mountainsides, not by machines, but by hands and time. These paths were more than routes; they were sacred zones where trade, spirituality, and wealth intertwined. If we rank the most valuable lessons humanity could inherit, understanding the land’s memory—its scars and its strengths—should top the list. Future generations will face climate crises and resource wars, but they will be better equipped if they know how to read the land as their ancestors did. The tale of the earth is not just about the past; it’s a manual for the future.

Topic: an essay discussing the ethics of using animals in medical research

Text:
The ethics of using animals in medical research is a complex and highly debated topic. While some argue that animal testing has led to groundbreaking discoveries—such as vaccines and life-saving drugs—others question whether the suffering caused to animals justifies these advancements. One brief but crucial aspect of this debate is the housing conditions of lab animals. Critics claim that many facilities fail to provide adequate space, leading to stress and poor health, much like prisoners in overcrowded jail cells. Supporters, however, argue that modern labs now prioritize independence in design, ensuring animals have more natural environments.

Another contentious issue is the flavour of research—whether scientists should focus solely on species that closely resemble humans, like primates, or expand to other animals. A recent study, refereed by leading experts, suggested that mice and rats, despite their small size, can still provide valuable insights. Yet, activists argue that even these animals suffer unnecessarily, comparing their plight to the parade of ethical concerns in human trials.

Cost is also a factor. The fare of conducting animal research is high, but proponents claim the long-term benefits—such as curing diseases—outweigh the expenses. Meanwhile, alternatives like computer models are improving, though they cannot fully replicate the complexity of living organisms. The debate remains unresolved, but one thing is clear: balancing progress with compassion is no easy task.
